[user] How do I transfer chats and data from Chat GPT to Claude?
[assistant] There's no direct "transfer" feature between ChatGPT and Claude — they're separate platforms that don't integrate with each other. However, here's what you can do:

**Export your ChatGPT data**

1. In ChatGPT, go to **Settings → Data controls → Export data**
2. You'll receive an email with a download link containing your conversations as JSON/HTML files

**Bring content into Claude**

Once you have your export, you can:

- **Copy/paste specific conversations** directly into a Claude chat if you want to continue a particular thread or analysis
- **Upload files** — you can attach exported files to a Claude conversation and ask Claude to read, summarize, or continue working from them
- **Summarize and recreate context** — paste in relevant parts of a past ChatGPT conversation and ask Claude to pick up from there

**What doesn't transfer**

- Chat history won't appear in Claude's sidebar the way it does in ChatGPT
- Any custom GPTs, memory, or personalization settings from ChatGPT have no equivalent import path into Claude
- Claude has its own memory/projects system that you'd set up independently

**Practical tip:** If you just want Claude to have similar context to what you built up in ChatGPT, the most effective approach is to write a brief summary of your project or ongoing work and paste it at the start of a new Claude conversation (or save it in a Claude Project if you're on a paid plan).

[user] Are there any existing All Domain Warfare Centres (or similar) within militaries, and how are they set up
Thought for 18s
Yes — there are a few real “all-domain / multi-domain” centres (and a larger number of close cousins that do the same job under different names: integration, C2, experimentation, warfare centres, joint exercise centres).
Below are credible existing examples and then a repeatable “how they’re set up” pattern you can lift.
Examples of existing All-Domain / Multi-Domain centres (or very similar)
1) National All-Domain Warfighting Center (NADWC) – Michigan National Guard (USA)
A purpose-built “all-domain” training construct that ties together a major training area (Camp Grayling) with an innovation node at an air base (Selfridge ANGB). It’s explicitly framed as an “All-Domain Warfighting Center” offering training to wider DoD entities.
Why it matters: this is one of the cleanest, most literal examples of what you’re asking for: a branded, standing “all-domain warfighting center” rather than a concept.
2) NATO Joint Warfare Centre (JWC) – Stavanger (Norway)
NATO’s JWC is a standing joint warfare and training institution that designs and runs high-end exercises with a “360-degree multi-domain setting” and is chartered to produce synthetic scenarios. They also explicitly position themselves as delivering multi-domain exercises and driving warfare development from concept into execution.
Why it matters: If you want a model for a joint / coalition all-domain centre that blends scenario design + experimentation + lessons capture, JWC is a mature reference point.
3) US Air Force Warfare Center (USAFWC) – Nellis AFB (USA)
USAFWC positions its focus as “All-Domain Air Force Warfighters” and sits above major operational test/training infrastructure (e.g., Red Flag ecosystem).
Why it matters: It’s a “warfare center” construct that’s training + tactics + test infrastructure, even if the service is air-focused.
4) US Air Force 505th Command and Control Wing (505 CCW) – operational-level C2 across domains
The 505 CCW is the USAF’s only wing dedicated to operational-level C2, specializing in C2 in air, space, and cyberspace and translating testing/tactics/training into operational C2 practice. (They are also tied to the USAF Warfare Center construct via reporting relationships.)
Why it matters: This is the “C2 integration engine” model — less about ranges and more about how decisions, effects, and information move across domains.
5) US Army Multi-Domain Task Force (MDTF) – not a centre, but the “integration nucleus”
MDTFs are described by CRS as the Army’s “organizational centerpiece” for multi-domain operations. In exercises, you see “hub” constructs emerge (e.g., Land Effects Coordination Center serving as an integrating C2 structure).
Why it matters: If your aim is an “all-domain warfare centre” that actually delivers effects, the MDTF pattern shows what the operational integration element looks like (as opposed to an academic/training centre).
6) UK Defence “Driving Integration” approach (Strategic Command as integrator)
The UK has framed this as an integration imperative inside doctrine, with Strategic Command responsible for implementing the change (even as terminology evolves). The earlier Joint Concept Note on Multi-Domain Integration (JCN 1/20) is also a useful conceptual anchor for how they thought about organising integration out to 2030+.
Why it matters: This is a model where the “centre” may be less a single building and more a defence-level integrator with federated nodes.
7) Naval Information Warfare Center (NIWC) Atlantic – “all-domain naval integration” experimentation
NIWC Atlantic describes running a large “System of Systems Naval Integration Experiment” to advance joint all-domain warfighting concepts.
Why it matters: This is the “integration experiment” centre-of-gravity model — event-driven, technically heavy, designed to connect sensors/effects/C2 across systems.
How these centres are typically set up (the common architecture)
Across the examples above, you’ll usually find one of three archetypes — many organisations combine two.
Archetype A: Training / exercise / scenario centre
Purpose: rehearse multi-domain operations, validate plans, build headquarters teams, generate lessons learned. Looks like: NATO JWC; parts of USAFWC; NADWC.
Typical setup

* Command group: Director/Commandant + small joint staff
* Scenario & wargaming division: scenario writers, red team/OPFOR, scripting, sim integration
* Exercise division: event design, control (EXCON), white cell, assessments
* Lessons / doctrine link: captures TTPs and feeds doctrine/training pipelines
* Tech backbone: sims, networks, instrumentation, data capture
Archetype B: Operational integration / effects coordination “hub”
Purpose: synchronize sensors-to-shooters-to-effects across domains (real ops or high-fidelity exercises). Looks like: MDTF-style coordination hubs (e.g., LECC) and 505 CCW’s operational-C2 focus.
Typical setup

* Ops integration cell (core): joint fires, EW, cyber, space, ISR fusion, information ops, targeting
* Authorities & liaison model: embedded reps from each domain owner to reduce “permission latency”
* Battle rhythm: daily effects boards, targeting cycles, collection management sync, legal/policy checks
* Data / comms: common operational picture + data standards + cross-domain release processes
Archetype C: Experimentation / rapid capability development centre
Purpose: connect industry/tech to operators; run trials; “system of systems” integration events. Looks like: NIWC-style integration experiments; parts of NADWC’s innovation node.
Typical setup

* Experimentation office: test design, hypotheses, measures of performance/effectiveness
* Engineering & integration: software, networks, data, interoperability, digital twins
* Operational user group: end-users define problems and iterate quickly
* Governance: pathways to procurement / fielding / doctrine change
The “minimum viable” org design (what you’d stand up first)
If you were building an “All-Domain Warfare Centre” in a modern force, a proven starting point is:

1. Directorate + Governance

* Director, Deputy, Chief of Staff
* Steering board with domain owners (land/air/maritime/cyber/space/info) and acquisition/tech lead

1. 3 Core Divisions

* Exercise & Scenario Division (build reps + certify HQs) — JWC pattern
* Integration & C2 Division (effects coordination hub) — 505/MDTF pattern
* Experimentation & Prototyping Division (trial + integrate) — NIWC pattern

1. Federated “Nodes,” not one monolith

* A physical training/range node (where you can instrument and run reps)
* A C2 / digital node (where you can run integration, data, sims, AAR tooling)
That “two-node” approach is exactly what makes NADWC-like constructs practical: training space + innovation/C2 space under one brand.
If you tell me your intended primary outcome (training validation vs operational effects vs experimentation) and what domains you truly control vs must coordinate, I can sketch a tailored centre design (ORBAT, roles, battle rhythm, data/IT spine, and authority model) that fits your environment.

CONCEPT PAPER
Saeed Bin Tahnoun All-Domain Warfare Centre (SBT-ADWC)
1. Executive Summary
The Saeed Bin Tahnoun Desert Warfare School (SBTDWS) is already a mature centre for desert warfare training and capability development. It delivers individual and collective training up to company level, supports operational planning, and contributes to equipment and doctrine development.
In parallel, SBTDWS has initiated a range of Capability Development (CAPDEV) projects spanning aerial mobility, unmanned systems, logistics, camouflage, and survivability.
These activities collectively form the foundation of a future All-Domain Warfare Centre, but they currently operate as separate efforts rather than a unified system.
This concept proposes the development of:
Saeed Bin Tahnoun All-Domain Warfare Centre (SBT-ADWC)
A centre that integrates:

* Desert warfare training
* Multi-domain experimentation
* Capability development
* Tactical innovation
* Operational integration
The transformation would leverage the existing structure, personnel, infrastructure, and training programs, minimizing cost and organizational disruption.
2. Strategic Rationale
2.1 Future Operating Environment
Modern military operations require forces to operate simultaneously across:

* Land
* Air
* Information
* Electromagnetic
* Autonomous systems
* Distributed logistics networks
Future operations in desert and austere environments will require:

* Distributed operations
* Autonomous systems
* Low-signature mobility
* Sensor evasion
* Rapid logistics
* Multi-domain integration
Current training structures prepare forces for:

* Tactical desert warfare
But not fully for:

* Multi-domain desert operations.
2.2 Strategic Opportunity
SBTDWS already possesses the key components required for an All-Domain Warfare Centre:
Training Infrastructure
The school delivers:

* Individual desert skills training
* Collective training up to company level
* Mission-specific preparation
* Exercise support and evaluation
Few institutions possess this training depth in austere environments.
Capability Development Ecosystem
Existing CAPDEV initiatives include:

* Paramotor reconnaissance and mobility
* Unmanned Ground Vehicles
* Drone logistics
* Electric tactical mobility
* Multi-spectral camouflage
* Decoy systems
* Water generation
* Tactical CASEVAC platforms
This already represents a multi-domain experimentation base.
International Training Hub
SBTDWS already trains:

* UAE Forces
* Partner forces
* International forces
This provides a strong foundation for an internationally recognized warfare centre.
2.3 Strategic Outcome
Transformation into an All-Domain Warfare Centre would establish:
The region’s leading centre for multi-domain operations in desert environments.
3. Mission Statement
SBT-ADWC develops, integrates, tests, and trains forces for multi-domain operations in desert and austere environments.
4. Core Functions
The Centre will operate across four integrated functions.
4.1 Function 1 — Desert Warfare Training
Role
Provide individual and collective training for desert operations.
Activities

* Individual desert skills training
* Mission preparation
* Survival training
* Navigation training
* Tactical training
* Collective training
These activities are already delivered by SBTDWS.
No structural changes required.
4.2 Function 2 — Multi-Domain Integration
Role
Integrate effects across domains during training and experimentation.
Activities

* ISR integration
* Targeting integration
* Multi-domain planning
* Data integration
* Effects synchronization
This function does not currently exist.
It represents the key capability gap.
4.3 Function 3 — Capability Development
Role
Develop and test equipment and concepts.
Activities
Structured experimentation of:

* Autonomous systems
* Tactical mobility
* Camouflage systems
* Logistics solutions
* Survivability technologies
This formalizes existing CAPDEV activities.
4.4 Function 4 — Tactical Innovation
Role
Rapid prototyping and soldier-driven innovation.
Activities

* Rapid equipment trials
* Tactical experimentation
* Field-expedient solutions
* Soldier innovation programs
This function builds on current initiatives.
5. Proposed Organizational Structure
Command Group

* Commandant
* Deputy Commandant
* Chief of Staff
Desert Warfare Training Wing (Existing)
Responsibilities:

* Individual training
* Collective training
* Exercise support
No major changes required.

[user] What makes military strategists and great leaders effective at developing frameworks for effecting cultural change in order to overhaul and drive an organization forward?   What principles and frameworks apply here?  Now apply these insights into the development of the Desert Warfare School from a singular focused organisation into an All Domain Warfare Centre. It should look to leverage the existing structure and tasks, but they should not constrain the analysis 
Below I’ll do this in two parts:
Part A — What makes great military strategists/leaders effective at cultural change (principles + frameworks). Part B — Apply those insights to evolving the Desert Warfare School (DWS) into an All-Domain Warfare Centre (ADWC).
I’ll keep this structured, practical, and directly usable for strategy/decision briefs rather than abstract leadership theory.
PART A — What makes great military strategists effective at cultural change?
1. The core insight
The best military leaders don’t “change culture directly.” They change behaviour, incentives, power structures, and systems — and culture follows.
Culture is the result, not the starting point.
This is the single most important lesson.
2. Five traits of leaders who successfully transform organisations
(1) They define a clear strategic “North Star”
Great reformers create a compelling destination that is:

* Simple to understand
* Hard to argue with
* Ambitious but credible
* Operational, not rhetorical
Examples in military history:

* “AirLand Battle”
* “Network-centric warfare”
* “Mission Command”
* “Special operations as a strategic capability, not just tactical force”
For DWS → ADWC, this might be framed as:
“From a Desert Warfare School → to the region’s premier All-Domain Warfare Centre for experimentation, training, and operational innovation.”
(2) They separate identity from function
Many organisations get stuck because they say:
“We are a school, therefore we must only train.”
Great strategists ask instead:
“What are we trying to achieve? What role should this institution play in the force?”
This is critical for your case.
DWS today is seen as:

* A training school
* A course provider
* A tactical training institution
A strategist would ask:

* What does the Presidential Guard actually need from this institution in 2030?
* Should it still be “just a school”?
* Or should it be a strategic enabler for warfare development?
This is the mindset shift that unlocks ADWC.
(3) They reshape incentives before values
Classic mistake: Leaders try to “change mindset” through speeches and workshops.
Smart leaders instead:

* Change promotions
* Change reporting lines
* Change budgets
* Change who gets rewarded
* Change what success looks like
Then behaviour changes automatically.
For example:
If DWS is still measured only on:

* Course throughput
* Pass rates
* Training hours
Then no one will care about innovation or all-domain warfare.
But if new success metrics include:

* Number of experiments conducted
* New tactics validated
* New capabilities integrated
* Interoperability trials run
* Lessons learned exported to the force
Then the culture will change naturally.
(4) They design structures that force collaboration
Great military reforms redesign structures so that cooperation is unavoidable.
Framework: Structure drives behaviour more than leadership.
Applied to DWS:
Instead of separate silos like:

* Individual Training Wing
* Collective Training Wing
* Advanced Training Wing
A strategist might introduce cross-cutting “mission cells” such as:

* All-Domain Experimentation Cell
* Drone & Robotics Cell
* ISR & Targeting Cell
* Fires Integration Cell
* Joint Mission Planning Cell
These would pull people from across the school into shared problems.
Culture changes because people work together differently.
(5) They use pilots, not big-bang reforms
The best leaders don’t try to transform everything at once.
They run 90-day pilots that prove the model.
For DWS this might look like:
Pilot 1:

* Create a Drone Warfare Cell inside DWS
* Run real experiments, not classroom theory
Pilot 2:

* Create an All-Domain Mission Lab
* Test new ways of integrating ISR + fires + manoeuvre
Pilot 3:

* Host an annual “Presidential Guard Warfare Experimentation Week”
If pilots work → scale them.
3. Key frameworks that apply
A. Kotter’s 8-Step Change Model (military adaptation)
Translated for your context:

1. Create urgency → Explain why desert warfare in 2030 requires an all-domain approach.
2. Build a guiding coalition → Bring CDD, STC, key commanders, and senior DWS leaders into a reform group.
3. Develop a vision → DWS → ADWC.
4. Communicate the vision → Make it the core narrative of the school.
5. Empower action → Give DWS authority to experiment.
6. Create short-term wins → 90-day pilots.
7. Consolidate gains → Expand pilots into permanent structures.
8. Anchor changes in culture → Make all-domain warfare “how we do business.”
B. Mission Command as a cultural framework
Mission Command principles are powerful for organisational reform:

* Clear intent
* Decentralised execution
* Trust
* Freedom to fail and learn
Applied to DWS:
Instead of micromanaging instructors, leadership would say:
“Your mission is to advance all-domain warfare capability. How you do that is up to you.”
This alone would shift culture dramatically.
C. Systems Thinking Framework
Great reformers treat organisations as systems.
Key question for DWS:

* What inputs shape behaviour?
   * Budget
   * Reporting lines
   * Tasking
   * Assessment criteria
   * Career pathways
If these don’t change, culture won’t.
PART B — Applying this to DWS → All Domain Warfare Centre
Now let’s apply all of this directly to your situation.
I’ll propose a model that leverages what exists but is not constrained by it.
1. Reframing the identity of DWS
Instead of:
“Desert Warfare School”
Reframe as:
“Desert Warfare & All-Domain Warfare Centre (DWAWC)”
Purpose:

* Train forces in desert warfare
* Experiment with future warfare
* Integrate drones, ISR, fires, autonomy, and cyber/electronic warfare
* Serve as the PG’s tactical innovation engine
DWS stops being just a school — it becomes a capability developer and test bed.
2. New strategic role
The new mission could be:
“To prepare the Presidential Guard for current and future desert warfare through integrated training, experimentation, and rapid innovation across all domains.”
This keeps training central but adds innovation and experimentation.
3. How to leverage existing structure (smartly)
You already have wings. Instead of replacing them, repurpose them:
Individual Skills Wing → Human Performance & Modern Warfare Wing
Add to its remit:

* FPV drones
* Small UAS operations
* Counter-UAS basics
* Electronic warfare awareness
* AI-enabled decision tools
Same people, broader mission.
Collective Training Wing → All-Domain Operations Wing
This becomes the heart of ADWC:

* Combined arms exercises
* Drone-integrated operations
* ISR + fires + manoeuvre integration
* Autonomy trials (UGVs, resupply drones, etc.)
This is where real experimentation happens.
Advanced Training Wing → Warfare Innovation Wing
Instead of just “advanced courses,” this becomes:

* Experimentation lab
* Prototype tactics hub
* Lessons learned centre
* Red-teaming function
This is where new concepts are tested.
4. New cross-cutting cells (the real cultural shift)
Create four permanent cells inside DWS:
(1) All-Domain Experimentation Cell
Mission:

* Run trials

* Work with CDD & STC
* Bridge training and capability development
This is your innovation engine.
(2) ISR & Targeting Cell
Focus on:

* Drones
* Recon integration
* Sensor-to-shooter timelines
* Digital targeting workflows
This aligns directly with modern warfare.
(3) Fires Integration Cell
Integrate:

* JTAC
* Artillery
* Rockets
* Air support
* Precision strike
This turns DWS into a joint warfare centre, not just infantry training.
(4) Autonomy & Robotics Cell
Covers:

* UGV trials
* Tactical resupply drones
* Autonomous platforms
* Human-machine teaming
This aligns perfectly with your existing work.
5. New measures of success (the culture driver)
Shift KPIs from:

* “How many students trained?”
To include:

* Number of experiments conducted
* Number of new tactics developed
* Number of capabilities tested
* Lessons learned adopted by units
* Joint integration exercises delivered
If you change metrics, you change culture.
6. 90-day pilot to kickstart ADWC

Pilot A — Drone Warfare Week
DWS hosts a week where:

* Multiple PG units come to experiment with drones
* Live trials are conducted
* New tactics are tested

Pilot B — All-Domain Exercise
A live exercise that integrates:

* Infantry
* Drones
* ISR
* Fires
* UGVs
* Digital C2
Run it through DWS.
Pilot C — Innovation Sprint
Bring together:

* DWS instructors
* CDD
* STC
* Industry partners
Task them with solving one real operational problem in 90 days.
7. How this changes culture
Over time:

* DWS is no longer seen as “just a school.”
* It becomes “where new warfare is tested.”
* Instructors become innovators, not just trainers.
* The PG starts looking to DWS for ideas, not just courses.
That is real cultural transformation.

1) SENIOR DECISION BRIEF (EXECUTIVE VERSION)
Title: Evolution of DWS → All-Domain Warfare Centre (ADWC)
Problem Statement
Desert warfare is no longer primarily about manoeuvre and infantry tactics. Modern operations are shaped by:

* ISR dominance
* FPV and loitering munitions
* Counter-UAS
* Electronic warfare
* Autonomous systems
* Digital C2
* Joint fires integration
* Sensor-to-shooter speed
The current DWS model — primarily a training school — is necessary but insufficient to prepare the Presidential Guard (PG) for 2030 conflict.
Proposed Solution
Evolve DWS into an All-Domain Warfare Centre (ADWC) that:

1. Continues core desert training, AND
2. Becomes the PG’s hub for:
   * Tactical experimentation
   * Rapid prototyping
   * Live trials
   * Lessons learned
   * Integration of emerging capabilities
   * Cross-unit interoperability
Strategic Shift (One Line)
From: “A school that delivers courses.” To: “A centre that shapes how the Presidential Guard fights.”
New Mission
“Prepare the Presidential Guard for current and future desert warfare through integrated training, experimentation, and rapid innovation across all domains.”
Key Benefits

* Faster capability adoption
* Better integration between training and trials
* Stronger link between DWS, CDD, and STC
* More operationally relevant training
* Reduced risk when fielding new technologies
* Stronger joint integration
* Clear innovation pathway inside PG
Decision Required
Approve:

1. Re-designation: DWS → ADWC
2. Establishment of four cross-cutting cells
3. Authority for DWS to conduct tactical experimentation
4. 24-month phased implementation plan
2) CONOPS — ALL-DOMAIN WARFARE CENTRE (ADWC)
Purpose
The ADWC exists to:

* Train forces in desert warfare
* Experiment with emerging technologies
* Validate tactics
* Integrate new capabilities
* Accelerate learning across the force
It is both:

* A school, and
* A warfare innovation hub
Core Functions
ADWC will perform five main functions:
(1) Training
Deliver:

* Individual skills
* Collective training
* Advanced courses
* Specialist integration training
This remains the foundation of the institution.
(2) Experimentation
Run live, realistic trials including:

* FPV drones
* Counter-UAS
* ISR integration
* Autonomous systems
* Digital C2
* Joint fires
Trials occur in the training environment, not just in labs.
(3) Integration
ADWC becomes the place where different capabilities come together:

* Infantry + drones
* ISR + fires
* UGVs + dismounted troops
* EW + manoeuvre
* Air + ground coordination
(4) Lessons Learned
ADWC maintains a dedicated Lessons & Innovation Cell that:

* Captures findings
* Writes tactical notes
* Produces recommendations
* Feeds lessons back to units
(5) Capability Bridging (with CDD & STC)
ADWC sits between:

* STC (future horizon scanning)
* CDD (formal trials and acceptance)
ADWC conducts tactical field testing before formal acquisition.
How ADWC Operates (Day-to-Day)
Every year will combine three activities:

1. Core courses running as normal
2. Rotating experimentation cycles
3. Annual All-Domain Exercise
3) NEW ORBAT — DWS → ADWC
Below is a proposed ORBAT structure that leverages your existing wings but transforms their purpose.
Level 1 — Command
Commander ADWC Supported by:

* Deputy Commander (Training)
* Deputy Commander (Innovation & Experimentation)
Level 2 — Existing Wings (Repurposed)
A. Individual Skills Wing → “Human Performance & Modern Warfare Wing”
New responsibilities added:

* Basic FPV drone operations
* Counter-UAS fundamentals
* Electronic warfare awareness
* Digital navigation & C2 tools
* AI-assisted decision-making basics
Core desert skills remain.
B. Collective Training Wing → “All-Domain Operations Wing”
Becomes the heart of ADWC.
Conducts:

* Combined arms exercises
* Drone-integrated manoeuvre
* ISR + fires + manoeuvre integration
* Urban + desert hybrid scenarios

OP / STRATEGIC EFFECT 2. TACTICAL / STRIKE EFFECT 3. SPECIALIST / DISCREET EFFECT 4. FPV Drone training program using reserve brigade to eventually develop 10 man FPV Drone teams  - Identify and produce fielding plans for the capabilities (FIC, DLOD, etc) with a focus on training, infrastructure, maintenance, sustainment, etc - Two main areas of focus for the new wing / component of the school: 1. Below is a complete, structured concept you can use as the basis for the proposal to establish an All-Domain Technology & Innovation Hub (ATI-Hub) for the Desert Warfare School (DWS). This is written at a level suitable for presentation to a 1-star / 2-star command environment and can be lifted directly into a PPT or concept paper.
ALL-DOMAIN TECHNOLOGY & INNOVATION HUB (ATI-Hub) — DESERT WARFARE SCHOOL
1. Strategic Intent
The ATI-Hub will act as the principal driver of emerging capability innovation across the Desert Warfare School and, by extension, the wider Presidential Guard. It will accelerate the identification, development, testing, and fielding of cutting-edge systems—particularly autonomous UxS platforms—through a dedicated, resourced, and commander-led ecosystem.
Its establishment reflects the need to:

* Generate organic, sovereign-style capability without dependency on foreign primes.
* Deliver rapid prototyping cycles relevant to regional threats.
* Integrate civilian industry, academia, and experts without the constraints of a military camp.
* Provide clear pathways from concept → prototype → validated → scaled → operational.
* Increase operational readiness in UxS, FPV, and discreet strike capabilities.
2. Location & Structure
2.1 Off-Camp Co-Located Facility
Purpose-built innovation campus outside the military camp boundary to allow:

* Civilian scientists, engineers, and university partners to enter without military clearance issues.
* Tech startups, local fabrication labs, gaming/simulation firms, and coding experts to embed directly.
* Industry partnerships, shared labs, and IP-friendly collaboration.
* Faster procurement, experimentation, and commercial negotiation cycles.
The facility will be connected to DWS through:

* A secured shuttle access protocol for classified work.
* A classified annex (“black lab”) for sensitive prototyping and tests.
* A field testing range nearby for UxS trials.
3. Governance & Leadership
Innovation Command (IC)
A dedicated formation created under an OF-5 Commander (Innovation & Technology) who reports directly to DWS Commander.
Core responsibilities:

* Lead strategy, governance, capability development, and budget control.
* Accredit and approve capability transitions from prototype to operational.
* Ensure interoperability across the DWS wings (ISW, ATW, CTW).
* Manage the commercial and legal interface through handover (HOTO) to primes.
* Maintain relationships with QUDRA, EDGE, Marakeb, IGG, and international R&D partners.
Subcomponents

1. Research & Development Wing (RDW)
2. Test, Evaluation & Experimentation Wing (TEX-Wing)
3. UxS & Discreet Capabilities Wing (operational output + training)
4. FPV Reserve Brigade Program (10-man drone teams)
4. Process Framework
A clear, repeatable pipeline aligns to T&E → MVP → Ready Options → HOTO.
4.1 R&D Stage

* Identify problem statements.
* Ideation workshops with military + industry.
* 3D modelling, electronics, coding, AI integration.
* Prototype fabrication (3D printers, CNC, composites, additive manufacturing).
* Early lab trials (bench tests).
4.2 T&E Stage

* Controlled field tests using TEX-Wing.
* Data collection, safety assessments, threat modelling.
* Development of draft TTPs.
* Integration with DWS instructors.
4.3 MVP → Operational Options

* Rapid improvements and design iterations.
* Validate against target performance criteria.
* Produce 5–10 pre-production units.
4.4 HOTO to Prime

* When commercialisation is viable, handover IP, specs, and manufacturing package.
* Prime partner mass produces (100+ units).
* ATI-Hub retains rapid tweak/improvement capacity for future revisions.
5. Organic Production Capacity
The facility will possess internal capacity to produce up to 100 units per system, ensuring:

* Quick war-time supply without foreign dependency.
* Stockpiling of systems for resilience.
* Non-attributable systems with neutral component sourcing and non-national signatures.
* Flexible design variations for discreet, covert or overt missions.
This production capability will require:

* Electronics lab
* Composites workshop
* Battery & power systems lab
* Secure additive manufacturing suite
* On-site QA and certification cell
6. Initial Capability Focus – UXS Portfolio
A coherent family of UxS systems to align with operational needs:
6.1 Operational / Strategic Effect

* Long-range autonomous systems for persistent surveillance.
* High endurance UAS with AI-enabled routing.
* Stand-off strike prototypes using loitering platforms (non-attributable).
6.2 Tactical / Strike Effect

* Small loitering munitions for platoon/company employment.
* Autonomous resupply UGVs and UAS for desert environments.
* Mid-range FPV strike drones.
* Deployable tactical C2 nodes and mesh networks.
6.3 Specialist / Discreet Effect

* Ultra-low-signature surveillance drones.
* Disguised UAS/UGVs for covert monitoring of routes and compounds.
* Throwables, micro-UAS, and confined-space sensors.
* Counter-tracking, spoofed identifiers, and signature-reduced platforms.
6.4 FPV Drone Program
Designed in partnership with the reserve brigade to raise ten 10-man FPV teams, built on:

* 3-week intensive operator pipeline.
* Instructor cadre from Innovation Hub + external SMEs.
* Organic FPV drone production (50–100 units / batch).
* Standardised spare parts pool, repair kits, batteries, and tactical antennas.
* Integration into DWS collective exercises, platoon attacks, and urban training.
7. Fielding & Capability Integration (FIC / DLOD)
Doctrine & Concepts

* Develop new TTPs for UxS in desert warfare (deep desert, wadis, urban edges).
* Align with national joint doctrine.
* Integrate into DWS curriculum (ISW, ATW, CTW).
Organisation

* Create Innovation Command under OF-5.
* Create TEX-Wing, RDW, FPV Wing.
* Establish SME teams (engineers, data analysts, drone specialists).
Training

* New training pipelines:
   * UAS Basic Course
   * UAS Commander Course
   * FPV Operator
   * UxS Maintainer Course
   * UxS TTP Development Course
Materiel

* Procure initial 20–30 UxS prototypes.
* Establish fabrication labs with certified tools.
* Create resilience stockpiles of batteries, chips, motors, sensors.
Personnel

* Blend uniform, reserve, civilian, and contracted SMEs.
* Scientist categories: AI/ML, aerodynamics, EW, battery tech, coding, mechanical/electrical engineering.
Infrastructure

* Innovation Campus (off-camp)
* Classified annex
* Test range
* Drone net & obstacle courses
* Battery charging lab (safe-room compliant)
Sustainment

* Tech rotation system (retire/upgrade cycles every 6–12 months)
* Battery life management plan
* Repair workshops (10-minute field repair standards)
* HOTO with primes for long-term production
Command & Control

* Dedicated UxS operational C2 node
* Mesh integration with DWS battle labs
* Secure protocols for storing sensitive data
8. Two Main Functional Areas
8.1 Research & Development Wing (RDW)
Functions:

* Technology scouting & horizon scanning
* UAS/UGV design
* Prototyping & fabrication
* AI & autonomy integration
* Signature reduction
* Electronics testing
* Power system design
* Partnerships with academia/industry
* Maintaining an innovations “backlog”
Personnel:

* Lead Engineer (Civ Lead)
* 10–15 technical staff (ME, EE, software, composites)
* Contract SMEs (AI, EW, drone racing experts, ex-mil)
8.2 T&E / TEX-Wing
Functions:

* Tactical assessment of prototypes
* Live environment trials
* Evaluation metrics & safety
* TTP development
* Lessons-learned repository
* Publishing of T&E Reports
* Integration into courses and collective training
Personnel:

* Wing Commander (Maj)
* 6–8 Senior Advisors/Operators
* Data analyst & instrumentation tech
* Liaison officers to DWS wings
9. Outputs Over the First 24 Months

1. 10–15 prototype systems (air/ground options).
2. Publish first DWS UxS doctrine annex.
3. 3 FPV teams trained → scaling to 10 teams.
4. Establish “Drone Factory” with 100-unit production capacity.
5. Create full T&E certification framework.
6. Deliver two fieldable capabilities to Main Command.
7. Operational trials embedded into annual Desert Warfare Cycle.
10. Strategic Advantages

* Enhances national resilience by reducing foreign dependence.
* Accelerates capability generation timelines from years → months.
* Positions DWS as the premier innovation and UxS training centre in the region.
* Builds local Emirati technical skillsets.
* Creates an ecosystem for future capabilities (AI-enabled targeting, robotics, exosystems, sensors).
* Strengthens inter-agency and international partnerships.

Can you please review this against like organisations that other nations have developed and incorporate the best aspects of what they have done, what has worked, and issues they identified
You’re basically being asked to build your own SOFWERX / DIU / BattleLab hybrid for the desert. Nice problem to have. 😄 I’ll walk through what similar orgs overseas do, what works, what’s gone wrong, and then show how I’d upgrade your ATI-Hub concept to bake in the best bits and avoid the traps.
1. Who we’re “benchmarking” against
Closest analogues to what you’re proposing:

* SOFWERX (USSOCOM, USA) – off-base innovation hub run with DEFENSEWERX; high operator involvement, rapid prototyping, fabrication “foundry,” sprint events.
* Defense Innovation Unit (DIU, USA) – Pentagon org that fields and scales commercial/dual-use tech for DoD at “commercial speeds”; strong on contracting pathways and transition to programs of record.
* UK Defence BattleLab (Dorset) – physical hub at a civilian innovation park; co-location with MOD, DSTL, local government; access to land/air/sea ranges; very similar “off-camp” model.
* NATO’s DIANA – multi-site accelerator network for dual-use deep-tech start-ups; challenge programmes, accelerator cohorts, and a big emphasis on alliance-wide collaboration.
* DEFENSEWERX hub network – the umbrella under which SOFWERX and others sit; specialises in building innovation “ecosystems” that connect government problems with non-traditional suppliers.
These are pretty good proof that what you’re trying to do is achievable, especially with an off-camp site and a dedicated OF-5 commander.
2. What’s worked well elsewhere (and why)
2.1 Off-camp, accessible, ecosystem hubs

* SOFWERX & Defence BattleLab deliberately place facilities outside conventional bases, making it easy for industry, academics, and non-cleared specialists to walk in, hot-desk, and prototype.
* BattleLab specifically markets itself as a front door to Defence where “creative collisions” happen and where testing ranges (land/air/sea) are nearby.
Implication for you: your off-camp location is exactly in line with best practice. Double-down by:

* Building co-working and maker spaces that industry/startups can rent or use.
* Co-locating with a civilian innovation park or university satellite if possible.
* Marketing it as “front door to DWS problems” – not just a lab.
2.2 Operator-driven problem framing & rapid events

* SOFWERX runs Rapid Prototyping Events (RPEs) and similar sprints: short, intense cycles where operators bring real problem sets and work directly with SMEs and vendors to produce proof-of-concepts.
* These events tend to:
   * Keep innovation tied to real mission problems.
   * Build trust between operators and nerds.
   * Deliver visible outputs in weeks, not years.
Implication for you:

* Make RPE-style events a core mechanism, not a side activity (e.g. quarterly “Desert UxS Challenge Weeks”).
* Ensure operators from the Desert Warfare School are embedded in every project from day 1.
2.3 Strong transition & contracting machinery (DIU lessons)

* DIU’s whole value proposition is transitioning commercial tech into actual military use at scale, not just tinkering.
* They use flexible procurement tools (like Other Transaction agreements) and a deliberate “pipeline” mindset – identify problem → source commercial tech → prototype → transition to service/program of record.
* Recent GAO work points out they still struggle with measuring transition effectiveness and working cleanly with other innovation orgs, but the model of explicitly owning “transition” is critical.
Implication for you:

* Your T&E → MVP → Ready Options → HOTO to Prime pipeline is exactly the right idea.
* You should explicitly staff a “Transition & Commercialisation Cell” whose job is:
   * Pre-agreeing acquisition pathways with higher command.
   * Managing IP and export controls.
   * Preparing a “fielding package” for each system (technical data, training, DLOD, support concept).
2.4 Networked accelerators & challenge programmes (DIANA)

* DIANA runs multi-country challenge programmes in specified focus areas (energy, autonomy, contested EM environment, extreme environments, etc.), giving startups funding, mentorship, and access to test facilities.
* Key strength: network model – multiple accelerator sites and test ranges, one governance and brand.
Implication for you:

* Think of your ATI-Hub as “node 1” in a future network, not a single isolated lab.
* Build MOUs early with:
   * National universities and tech parks.
   * Regional defence companies and UAS/robotics actors.
   * International partners’ innovation hubs (including DIU-style outfits, some of which are now posting liaison officers overseas, including to the Middle East).
2.5 Deep prototyping capability on site

* SOFWERX’s Foundry is famous for full CAD → 3D printing → machining → welding → electronics → test in-house, allowing very fast cycles.
* BattleLab and similar hubs are embedded in tech parks with 5G, high-bandwidth data, engineering workshops, test infrastructure, so teams can go from idea to first flight/drive quickly.
Implication for you:

* Your goal to organically produce up to 100 units of any system is ambitious but very aligned with this.
* You’ll want:
   * Full drone and UGV fabrication line (frames, motors, flight controllers, RF links).
   * Electronics lab for custom boards.
   * Environmental and endurance test rigs tailored to desert conditions.
3. Common failure points / issues others have hit
3.1 The “Valley of Death” – stuck between prototype and real fielding

* Even DIU has been criticised for not always getting technologies across the line into sustained programs, partly due to unclear metrics, limited buy-in from the traditional acquisition system, and fragmented collaboration with other innovation orgs.
Mitigation for you:

* Make each project start with a named “sponsor unit” and “adoption pathway” (e.g. “CTW will own this system if it works”).
* Build a formal “Acceptance to Service” gate with DLOD/FIC criteria signed by higher command.
3.2 Culture clash: operators vs tech vs bureaucracy

* Innovation hubs repeatedly report that cultural issues (suspicion of outsiders, fear of failure, rigid risk aversion) slow things more than technology does. This is visible in broader DoD innovation commentary and MITRE studies on rapid acquisition barriers.
Mitigation:

* Choose staff who are bilingual in both worlds (ex-operators with engineering interest, and engineers who can live in the field).
* Make “fail fast, learn fast” a command-endorsed principle with low-stakes experimentation where early failures are expected.
3.3 Metrics & performance management

* GAO observations on DIU highlight inconsistent performance metrics and difficulty proving impact to Congress/leadership.
Mitigation:

* From day one, define a small, ruthless set of key metrics, e.g.:
   * of projects reaching field trials per year.
   * Time from idea → first prototype → operational trial.
   * of systems transitioned to a unit with full DLOD package.
   * Operator satisfaction and adoption rate.
   * Cost per capability vs imported alternatives.
3.4 IP, attribution & export controls

* Dual-use accelerators like DIANA and DIU frequently wrestle with IP, export control, and dual-use complications – especially for autonomy, UxS, and EW.
Your angle:

* You want non-attributable systems and ideally no obvious national signature. That’s achievable, but you’ll need:
   * Early legal and compliance advice baked into the process.
   * Clear agreements on whether IP sits with the state, the hub, or partners.
   * Controls around what tech can/can’t be exported, or given to proxies.
4. Upgrades to your ATI-Hub design using these lessons
Below is a refined version of your concept, incorporating the best of SOFWERX/DIU/BattleLab/DIANA.
4.1 Governance & structure
Keep:

* OF-5 Commander Innovation & Technology.
* Off-camp facility.
* Two key functional pillars: R&D Wing and T&E / TEX-Wing.
Add / refine:

1. “Front Door to Defence” function
   * A small Engagement Cell that:
      * Runs industry days, challenge calls, and onboarding.
      * Manages NDAs, security briefs, basic due diligence.
      * Owns the external website/portal and application process.
2. Transition & Commercialisation Cell (inside Innovation Command)
   * Handles HOTO to primes, licensing, IP, and contractual routes.
   * Coordinates with national acquisition authorities so nothing is “orphaned”.
3. Advisory Board
   * Mix of: DWS command, national defence procurement, industry, academia, and maybe liaison from a friendly foreign innovation org.
   * Meets quarterly to review pipeline, kill/continue decisions, and risk.
4.2 Operating model – events and pipeline
Original: T&E – MVP – Ready Options – HOTO to Prime.
Upgraded, borrowing from SOFWERX/DIU/DIANA:

1. Problem Definition Phase (“User Challenges”)
   * Biannual call for problems from DWS (and wider command):
      * e.g. “How might we increase lethal and non-lethal UxS effects in deep desert out to 40 km?”
   * Each problem gets:
      * A Problem Sponsor (e.g. CTW CO).
      * An Operator Champion (SNCO or junior officer).
2. Sprint / Event Phase
   * Run themed Innovation Sprints:
      * “Desert FPV Strike Sprint”
      * “Autonomous Resupply in Extreme Heat”
   * Each sprint is 3–5 days, with:
      * Operators, industry, students, SMEs on site.
      * SOFWERX-style rapid prototyping in the Foundry-like workshop.
3. Prototype & TEX Phase
   * The top 1–3 concepts per sprint are selected for 3–6 month prototype projects.
   * TEX-Wing plans formal trials with objective data collection, safety envelopes and TTP development.
4. Transition Gate
   * Once a capability meets specified criteria:
      * DLOD/FIC package prepared.
      * Sponsor unit formally accepts or declines.
      * If accepted → move to 0–100 unit production and/or prime HOTO.
5. Portfolio Management
   * Maintain a portfolio view: Operational/Strategic UxS, Tactical/Strike, Specialist/Discreet, FPV training – each with a small number of active projects.
4.3 FPV Drone program – shaped by “what’s working” elsewhere
Keep:

* Reserve brigade as force base.
* Aim for ten 10-man FPV teams.
* Organic production of airframes.
Enhance with best practice:

* Build it as a formal centre of excellence under the ATI-Hub:
   * House an FPV Schoolhouse that:
      * Trains operators, maintainers, and planners.
      * Runs regular FPV “Red vs Blue” exercises as part of DWS courses.
* Introduce a competitive element (leaderboards, trials) to accelerate TTP development – SOF and FPV communities thrive on this model.
* Capture all learning in a living FPV TTP publication, updated after each exercise and trial.
4.4 Fielding & DLOD/FIC – making it real
For each new UxS capability, require a Fielding Dossier that includes:

* Doctrine & TTPs – from TEX-Wing (how to employ, integrate with fires, C2, EW, etc.).
* Organisation – which unit owns what (e.g. FPV team under Coy HQ, UGV det under Support Coy).
* Training – pipeline, refresher, simulation support, and qualification standards.
* Materiel – standard parts, spares kits, battery policies, packaging & transport.
* Infrastructure – needed charging stations, workshops, storage & test lanes.
* Personnel – MOS/role descriptions, manning deltas.
* Sustainment – repair times, stock levels, upgrade cycles, tech refresh triggers.
* C2 & Information – how feeds are integrated into command posts, legal/ROE issues, and data storage.
That’s where you leapfrog a lot of other hubs: most don’t fully own the DLOD/FIC thinking – they leave it to someone else and that’s where momentum dies.
4.5 International networking – learning from DIU & DIANA

* DIU is pushing liaison officers into allied countries (including the Middle East) to deepen tech collaboration.
* DIANA is building a distributed network across multiple NATO nations to support dual-use startups and share knowledge.
For you:

* Build into your concept that the ATI-Hub will:
   * Host international innovation days for friendly nations.
   * Exchange liaison officers/SMEs with partner hubs (e.g. a DIU/DEFENSEWERX or DIANA site).
   * Co-run at least one joint UxS / FPV challenge every 2–3 years.
5. What I’d present upwards as your “edge”
If you’re pitching this to higher command, your differentiator vs other nations’ hubs could be:

1. Focused operational domain – desert warfare, UxS, FPV and discreet capabilities in extreme heat and complex terrain.
2. Full DLOD / FIC ownership – you don’t just prototype; you deliver fieldable, doctrinally integrated capabilities.
3. Organic 0–100 unit production for non-attributable UxS systems – giving strategic autonomy and resilience.
4. Reserve Brigade integration – turning a latent manpower pool into a standing FPV and UxS talent pipeline.
5. Off-camp but command-led – best of BattleLab and SOFWERX, anchored firmly under an OF-5 Innovation Commander tied into DWS.
If you like, next step I can rewrite your original ATI-Hub concept paper in a way that explicitly references these international lessons (without naming specific foreign organisations in the version you show to seniors, if you prefer). I can also sketch a 24-month phased implementation plan that shows how you grow from initial UxS/FPV focus into a broader all-domain hub.

Below is a fully rewritten, polished ATI-Hub Concept Paper for the Desert Warfare School (DWS), integrating the best practices, lessons learned, and known pitfalls from comparable international organisations (SOFWERX, DIU, NATO DIANA, UK Defence BattleLab, DEFENSEWERX network).
This version is written as if for senior command review—professional, concise, strategically grounded, and focused on regional relevance. (It does not reference any foreign organisation by name, only their practices, so it is suitable for Command-level submission.)
ALL-DOMAIN TECHNOLOGY & INNOVATION HUB (ATI-Hub)
Concept Paper – Desert Warfare School
1. Purpose
The ATI-Hub will establish a dedicated, commander-led innovation and emerging-technology capability that accelerates the development, testing, and fielding of operationally relevant systems for the Desert Warfare School (DWS). It will combine research, rapid prototyping, tactical evaluation, FPV/UxS capability generation, and accelerated fielding pathways, ensuring the School remains the regional leader for integrated desert warfare and autonomous systems.
The concept draws on proven international models of military innovation hubs, incorporating what has worked, while avoiding cultural, bureaucratic, and transition failures observed elsewhere.
2. Strategic Rationale
Modern conflict demonstrates that technological advantage is no longer solely held by major powers; small, agile actors can leverage autonomous systems, FPV drones, AI-enabled sensors, and rapid prototyping to create disproportionate effect.
DWS must therefore shift from being a training institution to becoming an ecosystem for continuous capability generation—creating practical combat multipliers that are tailored to the desert environment and rapidly fieldable across the force.
The ATI-Hub will:

* Enable rapid innovation cycles measured in weeks/months, not years.
* Strengthen national resilience and autonomy by generating sovereign-style systems.
* Support the School’s role in shaping wider force design (2030 and beyond).
* Enhance partnership with industry, academia, and international allies.
* Create a regional centre of excellence for UxS, FPV, and discreet effect systems.
3. Governance & Leadership
Innovation Command
A new formation under uniform OF-5 leadership, reporting directly to the DWS Commander. Its mandate is to direct, coordinate, and certify all innovation, prototyping, and UxS capability development.
Three key subcomponents

1. Research & Development Wing (RDW) – Civilian engineers, scientists, technicians, and SMEs. – Responsible for design, prototyping, electronics, AI/autonomy integration, and fabrication.
2. Test, Evaluation & Experimentation Wing (TEX-Wing) – Senior advisors, operators, TTP developers. – Conduct live trials, tactical assessments, safety envelopes, and lessons capture.
3. UxS & FPV Capability Wing – Builds and trains operational UxS and FPV teams. – Delivers TTPs, sustainment plans, and fielding packages across DWS.
Supporting Cells

* Transition & Commercialisation Cell Handles contracting, IP, non-attribution design, HOTO to primes, and acquisition pathways.
* Engagement & Ecosystem Cell Runs innovation challenges, industry days, university partnerships, and international liaison.
* Advisory Board (Quarterly) Multi-domain oversight: command, acquisition, industry, academia, legal, and technical.
4. Facility Concept
The ATI-Hub will be located outside the military camp, enabling:

* Easy access for civilians, startups, and international partners.
* Faster contracting, procurement, and collaborative development.
* A “front door” for defence problems and challenge-based innovation.
Core infrastructure

* Advanced fabrication laboratories (3D printing, composites, CNC, electronics).
* AI/ML development suite.
* Prototype assembly bays for UAS/UGV.
* Desert-specific environmental testing rigs.
* Secure classified annex for sensitive projects.
* FPV drone training lanes, nets, and tactical ranges.
* Co-working and partner innovation spaces.
5. Operating Model (Best Practice-Driven)
International experience shows that the most successful hubs use structured, repeatable cycles focused on real operator needs, rapid prototyping, and deliberate transition mechanisms.
5.1 Phase 1 – Problem Definition (User Challenge Model)
Twice yearly, units across DWS and higher command submit prioritised problem statements. Each receives:

* A Problem Sponsor (unit commander).
* A User Champion (SNCO / junior officer) embedded with RDW teams.
This ensures projects solve real operational needs, avoiding the common failure of pursuing “interesting technology with no customer.”
5.2 Phase 2 – Sprint Events
Short, intense innovation sprints where operators, engineers, and industry co-develop early concepts.
These events create:

* Shared understanding of tactical needs.
* Rapid convergence on viable solutions.
* Visible momentum for command.
This method has been repeatedly proven as the fastest way to generate meaningful prototypes.
5.3 Phase 3 – Prototype Development (First 90 Days)
The RDW produces functional prototypes using integrated fabrication workshops. Cycles are intentionally short to maintain focus and avoid bureaucratic drag.
Outputs include:

* 3D models
* Electronics boards
* Flight control / autonomy integration
* First-flight / first-drive prototypes
5.4 Phase 4 – Tactical Evaluation & TTP Generation
The TEX-Wing conducts structured field trials:

* Environmental and endurance testing
* Failure modes & safety validation
* Tactical employment experiments
* Integration with fires, ISR, and C2
Every trial produces a TEX Report, which contributes to a continuously updated DWS UxS Doctrine Annex.
5.5 Phase 5 – Transition Gate
What differentiates successful hubs internationally is a clear handover mechanism. The ATI-Hub will use a formal gate requiring:

* Sponsor unit acceptance
* Compliance with DLOD/FIC requirements
* A fielding dossier (doctrine, training, sustainment, infra, organisation)
* Cost and production readiness
If successful, the capability proceeds to:
5.6 Phase 6 – Production (0–100 units)
The ATI-Hub will maintain the ability to produce up to 100 units of any given system, allowing:

* Rapid fielding
* Stockpiling for contingency
* Signature management & non-attribution
* Early iteration before a prime takes over
5.7 Phase 7 – HOTO to Prime / Larger Scale Manufacture
Once validated, the Transition Cell handles commercialisation and licensing for a partner company to scale production.
6. Initial Capability Focus (24-month Horizon)
6.1 Operational / Strategic UxS

* Long-range autonomy
* Discreet surveillance platforms
* Persistent overwatch systems
* High-endurance desert UAV
6.2 Tactical / Strike UxS

* Loitering-type effects
* Mid-range FPV strike platforms
* Autonomous desert resupply UGVs
* Mesh-network tactical comms nodes
6.3 Specialist / Discreet Effect Systems

* Ultra-low-signature drones
* Concealed UGV sensors
* Micro-UAS and throwable systems
* Non-attributable strike/surveillance payloads
6.4 FPV Drone Wing (Reserve Brigade)
A structured program to build ten 10-man FPV teams, with:

* Standardised FPV syllabus
* Organic drone production capability
* Intensive simulation-based training
* Repair workshops and sustainment packages
* Frequent “Red vs Blue” tactical FPV trials
* FPV integration in desert tactical exercises
This will be a regional centre of excellence.
7. Fielding Framework (DLOD / FIC)
Each capability will be delivered with:

* Doctrine: TTPs & integration with DWS curriculum
* Organisation: Unit roles, structures, ORBAT impact
* Training: Courses, standards, instructor qualifications
* Materiel: Parts, batteries, components, repair kits
* Personnel: Skill sets, MOS implications
* Infrastructure: Charging, storage, net areas, workshops
* Sustainment: Maintenance schedules, spares, refresh cycles
* C2 / Information: Data links, security, ROE, storage policies
This ensures capabilities are fieldable, not just experimental.
8. Key Lessons Incorporated (What Others Learned the Hard Way)
8.1 The “Valley of Death” problem
Many hubs fail between prototype and adoption. Our mitigation: formal transition gates, sponsor ownership, and full DLOD coverage.
8.2 Cultural barriers
Operators and engineers often speak different “languages.” Our mitigation: embed operators in every project from day one.
8.3 Lack of metrics
Success becomes anecdotal without measurement. Our mitigation: simple, ruthless KPIs:

* Time to prototype
* Time to field trial
* Systems transitioned
* Operator adoption rates
* Cost per capability
8.4 Bureaucratic slowdown
Traditional military processes suffocate innovation. Our mitigation: off-camp location, flexible engagement, rapid contracting pathways, and frequent sprint events.
8.5 IP & attribution issues
Tech becomes unusable if tied to foreign suppliers or national signatures. Our mitigation: early legal guidance, controlled supply chains, and in-house production.
9. Advantages for DWS and the Nation

* Sovereign-style capability in critical UxS domains
* Faster fielding than conventional acquisition processes
* A regional leadership position in autonomous desert warfare
* A national pipeline of engineers, operators, and technologists
* Strengthened partnerships with industry, universities, and allies
* Higher readiness, adaptability, and lethal/non-lethal options
* Lower dependence on foreign primes
* Credible contribution to future joint-force design
10. Recommended Next Steps (Next 120 Days)

1. Confirm OF-5 appointment & governance framework
2. Select off-camp site and design facility schematics
3. Establish Engagement & Transition Cells
4. Develop first set of User Challenges
5. Run inaugural Desert UxS Sprint Week
6. Build staffing model (uniform + reserve + civilian)
7. Identify early industry/academic partners
8. Stand up FPV training lanes & first instructor cadre
9. Begin procurement of fabrication & test equipment
10. Publish ATI-Hub Charter & metrics framework

✔ A detailed 24-month phased implementation plan

✔ A polished command-level concept brief (5–7 slides)

Ok, hold that thought. Before we move on please consider this expert advice about the establishment of an organisation / capability like this. Once understood, I would like you to incorporate his thoughts on how we can develop this idea further:  Firstly, here's a few thought provokers as you look at setting up:  1. What problem are you trying to solve? CAPDEV and S&T are two different beasts. Also, if you want an Innovation capability, or an R&D capability the same applies. My experience sits with the application of innovation which has focussed on the three horizons of innovation (extend core business, emerging tech and new with a 70/20/10 split in focus).   If you're able to identify the problem to be solved, then the deliberate first steps will ensure you start down the right path. Which, for any organisation, isn't necessarily easy or straight forward.   2. Are there any pre-existing frameworks, SOPs, strategy docs for you to align with? The answer to this will give weight to where effort is spent in either aligning/conforming to strategy or having to craft your own. My experience has seen us conform to strategy at both political, strategic and operational levels to ensure maximum support (which isn't always easy as you know).   3. The space. Is there a facility, hub or centre where this capability (the human aspect) will reside? A physical place of work that inspires the mindset to create change and drive the application of innovation is as equally important as the people leading it. If you just want a project management or trials capability, this is less important. If you want an innovation capability it is critical. This applies to the digital environment to capture, curate and monitor innovation projects. Again, important but not critical for trials etc.   4. The people and culture. Have the right people been chosen to lead and grow this? Does it have support from the top? For a long lasting capability you can't rely on one or two innovation champions, absolutely start with that as best possible but for long term success you'll need to grow a pipeline of intrapreneurs to inspire the right culture. This talks to the education requirements on how to execute innovation. If it's just trials, then a blended project management and technical workforce will get you by. Think things like design thinking, agile and lean methodologies. Without understanding the organisation in entirety, this might not be as applicable. However, if the requirement for creativity exists then it absolutely applies.   5. Have you determined the focus area? A blend between organisational change, technology and people should be considered. If you just focus on one it wont last, or reach its full potential. As an example, if you want a new drone, what does it mean for training and organisational design? Thinking of these things early, and identifying a clear technology vector would be strongly recommended.   6. Connectors. Have you got the right buy in from the top? There are a bunch of different ways to connect the bottom and the top, and both need to be connected to resources? This would have to be unpacked depending on the type of capability, and the level at which it operates (unit vs enterprise as an example).  Without going into the full back story of IXG, the key insights that may help are:  1. It is a pathfinding capability, and doesn't replace traditional procurement models.   2. It operates as a system, each sub element operates in support of the other. This allows transition points from idea to capability, and facilities the execution of innovation. Always sticking to an outcome-driven approach.   3. It is both flat and vertically aligned. Ideas can come from anywhere, and deliberate oversight ensures the support is facilitated to take an idea through to something meaningful.   4. It is a people capability. Our value is end user input, early and at speed. It is difficult to compare to any other organisation, where we are essentially problem owners as well as solvers. This drives a high sense of purpose in seeing things through.   5. Patience, persistence and perseverance are key principles. Operating in a large beaurocracy, you need all three. Some decisions are made quick, most are made slow. Having a workforce that can maintain a highly efficient tempo, immense drive coupled with a high degree of patience is critical. This will avoid the sense of disappointment or dissatisfaction for unfavourable outcomes, which are likely.   Mate, hope the short insight and questions help. Ultimately it comes back to what you want to do. Having a capability that can do prototyping, trials, experimentation and ideation is a big committment. If it's a smaller team, narrowing the focus to demonstrate some quick wins would be where I'd start (just like back in 2018/19).

I’ll break this into two parts:

1. Understanding the Expert’s Key Insights (Explained Clearly)
Below I translate each of his points into what he’s really telling us, and why it matters.
1.1 “What problem are you trying to solve?”
This is the single most important question in innovation.
He’s warning that CAPDEV, Science & Technology, R&D, and Innovation are NOT interchangeable:

* CAPDEV = structured capability development with doctrine, DLOD, deliberate acquisition.
* S&T = long-horizon scientific exploration.
* R&D = technical development and prototyping.
* Innovation = solving problems using new methods, usually with fast cycles.
He’s saying:
➡️ Until the ATI-Hub clearly defines the problem(s) it is solving, it will drift, become unfocused, or turn into a “project hub with gadgets.”
He also references the Three Horizons Model (70/20/10):

* Horizon 1 (70%): Improve existing capability
* Horizon 2 (20%): Emerging tech that could disrupt
* Horizon 3 (10%): New, revolutionary concepts
He is subtly advising:
➡️ If you don’t structure innovation across all three horizons, you will always “chase shiny objects” or be trapped in short-term tasks.
1.2 “Align with existing frameworks & strategy”
He’s highlighting a common failure of new organisations:
➡️ New hubs try to build their own doctrine from scratch instead of aligning to political, strategic, and operational direction.
He warns that without alignment:

* It will have no authority
* It will fight the bureaucracy
* It will lack funding or legitimacy
* It will be isolated from decision-making
1.3 “The space builds the culture — it must inspire.”
He is saying that if you want true innovation, not just “testing,” then:

* A physical space that creates a sense of creativity, open thinking, and possibility is essential
* The digital environment (task boards, innovation portals, dashboards) is equally important
* If you only want testing/trials, a simple technical facility is fine
* If you want a living innovation ecosystem, the space must be intentionally designed
➡️ He is warning you not to build a sterile ‘lab’ just for engineering. The space must be a creative accelerator.
1.4 “People and culture determine the long-term success.”
He emphasises:

* Choosing the right early leaders
* Growing a pipeline of intrapreneurs (internal innovators)
* Education in design thinking, agile, lean
* Senior-level buy-in
* Culture that tolerates failure and ambiguity
He is telling you:
➡️ Innovation is not a technical capability; it is a people capability. If you don’t design the culture deliberately, it will fail slowly.
1.5 “Focus area must include technology, organisation, and people.”
He’s emphasising that:

* Technology alone creates no capability
* Training, roles, unit structure, C2, logistics, doctrine — all must change together
* Every new system requires an organisational adaptation
This integrates with DLOD/FIC thinking:
➡️ A drone without doctrine, training, sustainment, and organisational design is useless.
He insists this be built from Day 1.
1.6 “Connectors — linking bottom, middle, top”
He warns that:

* Ideas come from the bottom
* Resources come from the top
* Execution is in the middle
* Unless these are connected, the ecosystem will stall
This is profound: Innovation is a system, not a team.
1.7 Insights from his organisation (IXG)
He summarises what makes their model successful:
(1) Pathfinding — not replacing procurement
Innovation provides options and bridges to procurement; it doesn’t compete with it.
(2) System-of-systems approach
Different sub-elements (ideation, prototyping, trials, transition) must link seamlessly.
(3) Flat + vertical
Ideas from anywhere → governed by leadership → resourced deliberately.
(4) People first — problem owners and solvers
They don’t just “do innovation”; they own the user’s problem.
(5) Persistence
This is a 5–10 year journey full of setbacks. You need a culture built for tempered ambition, not frustration.

PGC EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
PG Innovation Command (PGIC) and ATI-Hub (Desert Warfare Execution Node)
Purpose
This paper seeks Presidential Guard Command (PGC) approval to establish a PG Innovation Command (PGIC) and, within it, an All-Domain Technology & Innovation Hub (ATI-Hub) as a Desert Warfare execution node. Together, these will form a command-led, enterprise innovation system that rapidly converts priority operational problems into tested, fieldable capability options, while remaining aligned to existing CAPDEV and acquisition pathways.
Problem Statement
The operational environment is evolving faster than traditional procurement and capability development cycles. While the Presidential Guard (PG) excels in training and operations, it currently lacks a permanent, integrated mechanism to:

* Translate priority operational problems into solutions at speed
* Experiment and learn safely in realistic conditions
* Bridge the gap between prototypes and fielded capability (“valley of death”)
* Leverage industry and non-military expertise without undue friction
* Retain sovereignty, discretion, and resilience in critical technologies (e.g., UxS/FPV)
This gap constrains agility, increases dependence on external vendors, and delays the delivery of relevant capability to the force.
Proposed Solution
Establish PG Innovation Command (PGIC) under PGC, with ATI-Hub as a core execution component focused on desert warfare, UxS, FPV, and discreet effects.

* PGIC (Enterprise Command): Sets priorities, governs the innovation portfolio, allocates resources, and authorises transitions to CAPDEV/acquisition.
* ATI-Hub (Execution Node): Designs, prototypes, tests, and validates solutions rapidly; develops TTPs and training integration; enables limited organic production (0–100 units) to support rapid fielding and discretion.
This model is pathfinding, not procurement: it de-risks options and hands validated solutions to established acquisition mechanisms.
Operating Model (What Will Change)

* Problem-led: Command-endorsed problem statements with named sponsors and operator champions.
* Three Horizons (70/20/10):
   * 70% improving current capability and training
   * 20% emerging technologies with near-term impact
   * 10% disruptive concepts for future advantage
* End-to-End Pipeline: Ideation → rapid prototyping → tactical evaluation → transition gate → organic pilot production → handover to CAPDEV/prime.
* People-Centric: Operators embedded with engineers; early end-user input at speed.
* Ecosystem-Enabled: Off-camp facility and controlled commercial language to enable industry and academic participation.
Why This Works

* Command Authority: Clear governance under PGC prevents isolation and accelerates transition.
* Speed with Control: Flat execution within ATI-Hub; vertical oversight through PGIC.
* Reduced Risk: Early trials, kill/continue gates, and full DLOD/FIC fielding packages.
* Sovereignty & Discretion: Organic pilot production supports resilience and non-attribution.
* Early Wins: FPV and UxS programs deliver visible outcomes within 6–12 months.
What PGC Is Being Asked to Approve

1. Establish PG Innovation Command (PGIC) under PGC.
2. Authorise ATI-Hub as a PGIC execution component (Desert Warfare node).
3. Approve a 24-month phased implementation (pathfinding → system build → scale).
4. Endorse delegated authorities for rapid prototyping, testing, and transition gates.
5. Direct alignment with existing CAPDEV and acquisition frameworks.
Success Measures

* Reduced time from problem to field trial
* Number of capabilities transitioned with full DLOD/FIC packages
* Operator adoption and satisfaction
* Cost and resilience benefits versus imported solutions
Decision
Approval enables PG to establish a durable, enterprise innovation system that delivers relevant capability at speed, strengthens sovereignty, and positions the Presidential Guard as a regional leader in modern desert warfare and autonomous systems.
