PG INNOVATION COMMAND (PGIC)
CONCEPT OF OPERATIONS (CONOPS)
1. Purpose
This Concept of Operations (CONOPS) defines the mission, roles, authorities, operating model, and interfaces of the PG Innovation Command (PGIC). PGIC is established to provide the Presidential Guard (PG) with a command-led, enterprise innovation system that rapidly converts priority operational problems into validated, fieldable capability options, while remaining aligned with existing capability development (CAPDEV) and acquisition frameworks.
2. Mission
PG Innovation Command (PGIC) conducts enterprise innovation governance and enables rapid capability pathfinding in order to deliver tested, operationally relevant options to the Presidential Guard at speed, enhancing force agility, resilience, and future readiness.
3. Command Intent
PGIC will:

* Operate as the central innovation authority for the Presidential Guard
* Enable speed, experimentation, and learning while maintaining command control
* Remain problem-led and outcome-driven, not technology-led
* Act as a bridge between operators, industry, academia, and acquisition authorities
* Protect innovation tempo from unnecessary bureaucratic friction
* Ensure innovations transition into real capability or are deliberately terminated
End State: A sustainable, enterprise innovation system that consistently delivers validated capability options to the force and informs future force design.
4. Scope
PGIC IS RESPONSIBLE FOR:

* Translating PGC intent into innovation priorities
* Governing innovation across the Three Horizons (70/20/10)
* Allocating resources to innovation execution components
* Establishing innovation processes, standards, and metrics
* Authorising experimentation, prototyping, and trials
* Managing transition to CAPDEV and acquisition pathways
* Maintaining enterprise visibility of innovation activity
PGIC IS NOT RESPONSIBLE FOR:

* Replacing formal procurement or acquisition authorities
* Conducting long-term scientific research detached from operational need
* Owning sustainment of fielded systems
* Acting as a standalone project office for non-priority initiatives
5. Command Relationships
5.1 Higher Command
PGIC operates under the command authority of Presidential Guard Command (PGC).
PGC provides:

* Strategic direction
* Innovation priorities
* Resource endorsement
* Final approval for major transitions and scaling decisions
5.2 Subordinate Elements
PGIC commands and enables innovation execution components, including:

* ATI-Hub (All-Domain Technology & Innovation Hub)
   * Desert Warfare innovation execution node
   * Focus on UxS, FPV, discreet effects, and desert-specific problems
Future innovation nodes may be established under PGIC as required.
6. Operating Concept
PGIC operates as a system-of-systems, ensuring that innovation is not episodic or personality-dependent, but deliberate, repeatable, and governed.
6.1 Problem-Led Innovation
All innovation activity begins with a command-endorsed problem statement, supported by:

* A named sponsor (unit/formation commander)
* A named operator champion
This ensures relevance, ownership, and accountability.
6.2 Three Horizons Framework
PGIC manages innovation effort across:

* Horizon 1 (70%) – Extend and improve current capability
* Horizon 2 (20%) – Emerging technologies with near-term impact
* Horizon 3 (10%) – Disruptive concepts for future advantage
This balance ensures immediate value while maintaining future relevance.
6.3 End-to-End Innovation Pipeline
PGIC governs the following pipeline:

1. Problem Definition
2. Ideation & Innovation Sprints
3. Rapid Prototyping (≤90 days)
4. Test, Evaluation & Experimentation (TEX)
5. Transition Gate (Kill / Continue / Transition)
6. Pilot Production (0–100 units, where required)
7. Handover to CAPDEV / Acquisition
PGIC retains authority to terminate projects early to conserve resources.
7. Authorities & Decision Rights
PGIC is delegated authority to:

* Approve innovation projects aligned to PGC priorities
* Allocate funding and resources within its mandate
* Authorise rapid prototyping, trials, and experimentation
* Approve kill / continue decisions
* Endorse transition to CAPDEV and acquisition authorities
PGIC does not independently approve long-term procurement or sustainment.
8. Interfaces
8.1 CAPDEV & Acquisition
PGIC interfaces closely with existing CAPDEV and acquisition bodies to:

* Ensure alignment with doctrine and force design
* De-risk future acquisition decisions
* Provide validated technical, operational, and sustainment data
8.2 Operational Units
PGIC ensures:

* Early and continuous operator involvement
* Field trials conducted in realistic conditions
* Feedback loops into doctrine, training, and organisation
8.3 Industry & Academia
PGIC enables controlled engagement with:

* Defence industry partners
* Start-ups and SMEs
* Academic and research institutions
Engagement is structured, outcome-driven, and governed by security and IP frameworks.
9. Risk Management
PGIC manages innovation risk through:

* Clear governance and authorities
* Early-stage experimentation and failure tolerance
* Formal transition gates
* Metrics-driven oversight
* Legal, IP, and export-control review where required
10. Measures of Effectiveness
PGIC success will be assessed through:

* Time from problem identification to field trial
* Number of capabilities transitioned to CAPDEV
* Operator adoption and satisfaction
* Cost, resilience, and responsiveness improvements
* Contribution to Force Design objectives
11. Cultural Principles
PGIC operates on the following principles:

* Problem ownership
* People-first innovation
* High tempo with disciplined patience
* Flat execution, vertical accountability
* Early learning, early decisions
12. End State
PGIC is fully integrated into the Presidential Guard’s command and capability ecosystem, delivering a continuous pipeline of validated, fieldable innovation outcomes that enhance operational effectiveness and future readiness.
Next Deliverable
👉 ATI-Hub CONOPS (Nested Under PGIC)
When ready, say “Go ATI-Hub CONOPS.”
Go ATI-Hub CONOPS
Understood. Proceeding with Deliverable 4.
Below is the ATI-Hub CONCEPT OF OPERATIONS (CONOPS), written as a nested execution CONOPS under PG Innovation Command (PGIC), in a military doctrinal tone with controlled commercial language. This is suitable for formal staffing, annexing, and downstream SOP development.
ALL-DOMAIN TECHNOLOGY & INNOVATION HUB (ATI-HUB)
CONCEPT OF OPERATIONS (CONOPS)
(Component of PG Innovation Command – PGIC)
1. Purpose
This Concept of Operations (CONOPS) defines the mission, scope, organisation, operating model, and interfaces of the All-Domain Technology & Innovation Hub (ATI-Hub). ATI-Hub is an execution component of PG Innovation Command (PGIC), responsible for rapidly translating PGIC-prioritised operational problems into tested, validated, and fieldable capability options, with a primary focus on desert warfare, UxS, FPV, and discreet effects.
2. Mission
ATI-Hub designs, prototypes, tests, and validates innovative capabilities in order to deliver operationally relevant, field-ready options to PGIC, enabling rapid adoption by the Presidential Guard while informing future capability development and force design.
3. Command Intent
ATI-Hub will:

* Operate as a high-tempo execution node within the PG innovation system
* Remain problem-led and user-centred at all times
* Integrate operators, engineers, and SMEs from project inception
* Accept early failure to accelerate learning and reduce downstream risk
* Deliver disciplined, evidence-based recommendations to PGIC
* Maintain focus on outcomes, not technology demonstrations
End State: ATI-Hub consistently delivers credible, validated capability options that can be rapidly fielded, transitioned, or deliberately terminated.
4. Scope
ATI-Hub IS RESPONSIBLE FOR:

* Rapid design and prototyping of systems and solutions
* Tactical experimentation and Test, Evaluation & Experimentation (TEX)
* Development of tactics, techniques, and procedures (TTPs)
* Integration of new capabilities into training and doctrine
* Limited organic pilot production (0–100 units) where required
* Initial fielding support and feedback capture
* Maintaining a desert-specific innovation knowledge base
ATI-Hub IS NOT RESPONSIBLE FOR:

* Enterprise prioritisation of innovation problems
* Long-term sustainment of fielded capabilities
* Formal acquisition or procurement decisions
* Strategic-level policy or doctrine development
5. Command Relationships
5.1 Higher Command
ATI-Hub operates under PG Innovation Command (PGIC), which:

* Assigns tasks and priorities
* Provides resources and authorities
* Approves transition, scaling, or termination decisions
5.2 Supported / Supporting Units
ATI-Hub supports:

* Desert Warfare School (DWS) wings
* Operational units assigned as sponsors
* Reserve formations contributing to FPV and UxS programs
ATI-Hub is supported by:

* PGIC governance and transition authorities
* CAPDEV and acquisition organisations (as required)
6. Organisation
ATI-Hub is commanded by an OF-5 Innovation Commander and structured to operate as an integrated system.
6.1 Core Sub-Elements
a. Research & Development Wing (RDW)

* Engineers, scientists, technologists, SMEs
* Design, modelling, prototyping, fabrication
* Autonomy, AI, power systems, signatures, materials
b. Test, Evaluation & Experimentation Wing (TEX)

* Senior operators and advisors
* Tactical trials, safety assessment, data collection
* TTP development and validation
* Lessons identification and archiving
c. UxS & FPV Capability Wing

* FPV operator and instructor training
* UxS operator familiarisation
* Team generation (incl. reserve integration)
* Training and exercise integration
d. Local Transition Cell

* Prepares transition packages for PGIC
* Manages IP, non-attribution considerations
* Coordinates pilot production and handover
e. Engagement & Ecosystem Cell

* Industry, SME, and academic engagement
* Innovation sprints and challenge events
* Partner onboarding and coordination
7. Physical & Digital Operating Environment
7.1 Physical Environment
ATI-Hub operates from an off-camp innovation facility designed to enable:

* Rapid collaboration with non-military partners
* Secure prototyping and experimentation
* FPV and UxS training lanes
* Fabrication, electronics, and AI labs
* A classified annex for sensitive projects
The facility is designed to inspire creativity while supporting operational realism.
7.2 Digital Environment
ATI-Hub maintains a digital innovation environment to:

* Capture and curate ideas and projects
* Track progress and decisions
* Archive lessons and TTPs
* Provide visibility to PGIC
8. Operating Concept
8.1 Problem Intake
ATI-Hub receives PGIC-approved problem statements, each with:

* A named sponsor
* A named operator champion
Problems are scoped for relevance, feasibility, and alignment to the Three Horizons model.
8.2 Innovation Sprints
ATI-Hub conducts short, focused innovation sprints involving:

* Operators
* Engineers
* SMEs and partners
Sprints aim to converge rapidly on viable solution concepts.
8.3 Rapid Prototyping
Selected concepts enter a ≤90-day prototyping cycle, producing:

* Functional prototypes
* Initial technical data
* Early operational insights
Early termination is encouraged where value is not demonstrated.
8.4 Test, Evaluation & Experimentation (TEX)
TEX-Wing conducts structured trials to:

* Validate performance and safety
* Explore tactical employment
* Develop and refine TTPs
Outputs include formal TEX reports and recommendations.
8.5 Transition & Fielding
ATI-Hub prepares a Fielding Dossier including:

* Doctrine and TTPs
* Training requirements
* Organisational implications
* Sustainment considerations
PGIC determines transition, scaling, or termination.
9. FPV & UxS Flagship Program
ATI-Hub will establish a FPV & UxS Centre of Excellence to deliver early operational value.
Key elements:

* Reserve-enabled FPV team generation (10 × 10-man teams)
* Organic drone production capability
* Instructor pipeline
* Red vs Blue experimentation
* Integration into DWS exercises
This program provides rapid credibility and continuous learning.
10. Risk Management
ATI-Hub manages risk through:

* Controlled experimentation
* Incremental development
* Early operator involvement
* Formal kill / continue gates
* PGIC oversight and assurance
11. Measures of Performance
ATI-Hub performance is measured by:

* Time from tasking to prototype
* Time from prototype to field trial
* Quality of TTPs and lessons produced
* Number of validated options delivered to PGIC
* Operator adoption and feedback
12. Cultural Principles
ATI-Hub personnel operate under the following principles:

* Problem ownership
* Mission focus
* Technical excellence
* Tactical credibility
* Persistence and patience
* Collaboration across boundaries
13. End State
ATI-Hub is a trusted, high-tempo execution component within PGIC, delivering consistent, operationally relevant innovation outcomes that enhance desert warfare capability and inform enterprise force development.

Go Governance Model
Understood. Proceeding with Deliverable 5.
Below is the PGIC–ATI Governance & Authority Model, written to be clear, enforceable, and defensible at Command level. This is the document that prevents confusion, overlap, and failure over time.
PG INNOVATION COMMAND (PGIC) – ATI-HUB
GOVERNANCE & AUTHORITY MODEL
1. Purpose
This Governance & Authority Model defines decision rights, responsibilities, oversight mechanisms, and escalation pathways between Presidential Guard Command (PGC), PG Innovation Command (PGIC), and ATI-Hub.
Its purpose is to:

* Enable speed without loss of control
* Prevent role ambiguity and duplication
* Protect innovation tempo while maintaining accountability
* Ensure consistent transition of innovation into capability outcomes
2. Governance Principles
The governance model is built on the following principles:

1. Command-led, not committee-led
2. Flat execution with vertical accountability
3. Problem ownership from start to finish
4. Early risk exposure and early decisions
5. Pathfinding, not procurement
6. Innovation as a system, not a project
3. Governance Layers
3.1 Presidential Guard Command (PGC)
Strategic Authority
PGC retains authority over:

* Strategic intent and priorities
* Establishment and continuation of PGIC
* Resource endorsement at enterprise level
* Approval of major transitions with strategic implications
* Risk acceptance for high-impact or sensitive capabilities
PGC does not manage individual innovation projects.
3.2 PG Innovation Command (PGIC)
Enterprise Governance & Control
PGIC is the central governance authority for innovation across the Presidential Guard.
PGIC holds authority to:

* Approve innovation problem statements
* Prioritise projects across the Three Horizons (70/20/10)
* Allocate funding, manpower, and facilities
* Task ATI-Hub and other execution nodes
* Approve rapid prototyping and experimentation
* Authorise kill / continue / transition decisions
* Endorse handover to CAPDEV / acquisition
* Manage enterprise innovation risk
PGIC is accountable to PGC for:

* Innovation portfolio performance
* Use of resources
* Transition outcomes
* Risk management
3.3 ATI-Hub
Execution Authority
ATI-Hub holds delegated authority to:

* Execute PGIC-approved tasks
* Conduct rapid prototyping and trials
* Engage industry, academia, and SMEs within approved frameworks
* Conduct innovation sprints and experimentation
* Recommend kill / continue / transition decisions
* Conduct limited organic production (0–100 units) when authorised
* Develop TTPs, training inputs, and fielding dossiers
ATI-Hub cannot:

* Set enterprise priorities
* Commit PG to long-term procurement
* Independently scale capabilities beyond delegated limits
* Engage in unapproved commercial arrangements
4. Decision Rights Matrix (Simplified)
Decision AreaPGCPGICATI-HubStrategic Innovation Priorities✔▲✖Approve Innovation Problems✖✔▲Resource Allocation▲✔✖Innovation Sprint Approval✖✔▲Rapid Prototyping✖✔✔Field Trials / TEX✖✔✔Kill / Continue Decisions✖✔▲Transition to CAPDEV▲✔✖Organic Production (0–100)✖✔✔Long-term Procurement✔✖✖
✔ = Authority ▲ = Input / Recommendation ✖ = No Authority
5. Innovation Priorities Board (IPB)
Role
The IPB is the primary decision forum under PGIC.
Composition

* Commander PGIC (Chair)
* Representative(s) from PGC
* CAPDEV / Force Design representative
* ATI-Hub Commander
* Legal / commercial advisor (as required)
Responsibilities

* Approve problem statements
* Review portfolio balance (Three Horizons)
* Approve kill / continue / transition recommendations
* Resolve resource conflicts
* Manage enterprise risk
Cadence

* Quarterly formal review
* Ad hoc reviews for time-sensitive issues
6. Transition Governance
6.1 Transition Gate Criteria
A project may transition only when:

* Sponsor unit endorses operational value
* TEX results demonstrate performance and safety
* DLOD / FIC considerations are addressed
* Sustainment concept is credible
* Legal, IP, and security considerations are resolved
6.2 Transition Pathways
Projects may:

* Transition to CAPDEV for formal acquisition
* Be handed over to a prime contractor
* Remain in limited operational use
* Be terminated with lessons archived
PGIC authorises the pathway.
7. Risk & Assurance Model
Risk Categories

* Operational
* Technical
* Financial
* Legal / IP
* Reputational
Controls

* Early experimentation
* Incremental scaling
* Formal decision gates
* Independent TEX input
* PGC escalation for high-risk items
8. Metrics & Reporting
PGIC reports to PGC on:

* Time to prototype
* Time to field trial
* Transition rates
* Portfolio balance (H1/H2/H3)
* Resource utilisation
* Operator adoption
* Lessons learned
ATI-Hub reports to PGIC monthly.
9. Escalation Pathways

* ATI-Hub → PGIC: execution risks, scope issues
* PGIC → PGC: strategic risk, resource constraints, high-impact decisions
* Direct escalation permitted for safety or security issues
10. Governance Failure Modes (Explicitly Addressed)
This model deliberately prevents:

* Innovation drift
* Project orphaning
* “Innovation theatre”
* Overreach into procurement
* Personality-driven outcomes
* Loss of command control
11. End State
PGIC and ATI-Hub operate within a clear, disciplined governance framework that enables rapid innovation while preserving accountability, authority, and strategic alignment.

👉 24-Month Phased Implementation Plan

Go 24-Month Plan
Proceeding with Deliverable 6.
Below is the 24-Month Phased Implementation Plan for PG Innovation Command (PGIC) and the ATI-Hub (Desert Warfare Execution Node). This is written to be realistic, defensible, and executable, explicitly reflecting the expert advice you provided (start small, pathfind, build credibility, then scale).
PG INNOVATION COMMAND (PGIC) & ATI-HUB
24-Month Phased Implementation Plan
Implementation Philosophy
This plan is deliberately phased, outcome-driven, and restrained.
It is designed to:

* Demonstrate early credibility and value
* Avoid over-commitment before processes mature
* Build people, culture, and systems before scale
* Allow leadership to pause, redirect, or accelerate at defined decision points
Innovation capacity is grown progressively, not declared complete on Day One.
PHASE 1 — PATHFINDING & CREDIBILITY (0–6 MONTHS)
“Prove the system works”
Objectives

* Establish PGIC and ATI-Hub as legitimate, command-endorsed entities
* Demonstrate rapid, visible outputs (“quick wins”)
* Validate governance, authorities, and operating rhythm
* Build trust with operators, commanders, and partners
Key Activities
1. Command & Governance Stand-Up

* Appoint PGIC Commander and core staff
* Appoint ATI-Hub Commander (OF-5) and initial leadership team
* Formalise PGIC Charter and ATI-Hub Charter
* Establish Innovation Priorities Board (IPB)
* Confirm delegated authorities and reporting cadence
2. Facility & Enablers

* Secure off-camp ATI-Hub facility (initial operating capability)
* Fit out minimum viable infrastructure:
   * Collaboration space
   * Basic fabrication (3D printing, electronics benches)
   * FPV training lanes (basic)
* Establish digital innovation environment (project tracking, reporting)
3. Problem Definition & Portfolio Selection

* PGC endorses 3–5 priority problem statements
* PGIC allocates problems across Horizons:
   * 2–3 Horizon 1
   * 1 Horizon 2
   * 0–1 Horizon 3
* Assign sponsor units and operator champions
4. Initial Innovation Sprints

* Conduct 2–3 innovation sprints
* Focus on:
   * FPV employment
   * Tactical UxS for desert operations
* Rapid convergence on viable concepts
5. FPV Pilot Program (Flagship Quick Win)

* Establish FPV Centre of Excellence (IOC)
* Select reserve brigade personnel
* Train 2 x 10-man FPV teams
* Begin organic FPV drone assembly
* Conduct Red vs Blue experimentation
Key Outputs (Phase 1)

* PGIC and ATI-Hub operational
* 2–3 functional prototypes
* FPV teams trained and operational
* First TEX reports and draft TTPs
* Demonstrated governance and kill/continue decisions
Phase 1 Decision Gate
PGC confirms:

* Continue as planned
* Adjust scope or focus
* Authorise expansion to Phase 2
PHASE 2 — SYSTEM BUILD & INTEGRATION (6–18 MONTHS)
“Turn activity into a repeatable system”
Objectives

* Institutionalise innovation processes
* Expand technical and human capacity
* Integrate outputs into training and doctrine
* Deliver first formal transitions to CAPDEV / acquisition
Key Activities
1. Capability & Workforce Expansion

* Expand RDW and TEX staffing
* Introduce formal innovation education:
   * Design thinking
   * Agile / lean
   * Innovation leadership
* Build intrapreneur pipeline within PG
2. Facility Enhancement

* Expand fabrication capability:
   * Advanced prototyping
   * Battery and power systems
   * Signature management
* Upgrade FPV and UxS training infrastructure
* Establish classified annex (as required)
3. Portfolio Growth

* Increase active projects to 6–10
* Balanced across all Three Horizons
* Introduce more complex UxS systems (air and ground)
* Increase experimentation realism and scale
4. Doctrine, Training & Integration

* Publish DWS UxS / FPV doctrine annex
* Embed ATI-Hub outputs into:
   * Individual Skills Wing
   * Collective Training Wing
   * Advanced Training Wing
* Develop instructor and maintainer pipelines
5. Transition & Fielding

* Prepare full DLOD / FIC fielding dossiers
* PGIC authorises first transitions to:
   * CAPDEV
   * Prime contractors
* Conduct pilot production runs (up to 100 units)
Key Outputs (Phase 2)

* Fully functioning innovation system
* Multiple validated capabilities
* First formal handovers to CAPDEV
* Doctrine and training updated
* Increased operational adoption
Phase 2 Decision Gate
PGC confirms:

* System maturity
* Readiness to scale
* Authorisation for Phase 3 expansion
PHASE 3 — SCALE, NETWORK & ENDURE (18–24 MONTHS)
“Move from success to permanence”
Objectives

* Scale innovation throughput responsibly
* Expand beyond desert warfare where directed
* Formalise international and industry partnerships
* Cement PGIC as an enduring command capability
Key Activities
1. Scale Production & Fielding

* Increase organic production tempo
* Support wider fielding of validated capabilities
* Establish refresh and upgrade cycles
2. Networked Innovation

* Establish additional PGIC innovation nodes (if directed)
* Conduct joint and partnered trials
* Formalise academic and industry partnerships
3. Institutionalisation

* Codify lessons learned into policy
* Refine governance and metrics
* Embed innovation into force design cycles
* Confirm long-term funding and staffing models
Key Outputs (Phase 3)

* Sustained innovation throughput
* Networked PG innovation ecosystem
* Multiple capabilities in service
* Recognised centre of excellence
End State (24 Months)
By Month 24:

* PGIC is an established enterprise command
* ATI-Hub is a trusted execution node
* Innovation is repeatable, not personality-dependent
* PG can generate, test, and field capability at speed
* The force is more agile, resilient, and future-ready

Proceeding with Deliverable 8.
Below is the FPV & UxS Program Annex, written as a formal annex to the PGIC/ATI-Hub CONOPS. It is operationally grounded, scalable, and designed to deliver early, visible combat-relevant outcomes while feeding the broader PG Innovation Command system.
ANNEX X
FPV & UxS PROGRAM
(PG Innovation Command – ATI-Hub Execution)
1. Purpose
This annex defines the structure, training pipeline, production model, sustainment concept, and employment framework for the FPV & UxS Program. The program is designed as a flagship, early-delivery capability under PG Innovation Command (PGIC), executed by the ATI-Hub, to rapidly generate combat-ready FPV and UxS teams for desert warfare and related operational contexts.
2. Program Intent
The FPV & UxS Program aims to:

* Rapidly generate trained FPV strike and ISR teams
* Establish an organic, resilient FPV production capability
* Develop validated TTPs through experimentation and exercises
* Integrate FPV/UxS effects into PG training and operations
* Provide a scalable model leveraging reserve forces
* Deliver credible capability within 6–12 months
This program prioritises speed, learning, and operational relevance over perfection.
3. Scope
In Scope

* FPV ISR and strike platforms
* Small UAS and selected UGV applications
* Operator and instructor training
* Organic assembly and limited production (0–100 units)
* Maintenance, repair, and sustainment at unit level
* Tactical employment and integration into exercises
Out of Scope

* Long-range strategic UAS
* Independent procurement decisions
* Long-term sustainment contracts
* Export or third-party transfer decisions
4. Command & Control

* OPCON: Supported operational commander (during ops)
* TACON: Supported unit / formation
* ADMIN / Training: ATI-Hub (FPV Centre of Excellence)
* Governance: PG Innovation Command (PGIC)
5. FPV Centre of Excellence (CoE)
Role
The FPV CoE, located within ATI-Hub, is the authoritative body for:

* Training standards and certification
* Instructor qualification
* TTP development and validation
* Lessons learned and doctrine updates
* Safety and risk controls
Core Functions

* Run initial and refresher courses
* Support exercises and trials
* Evaluate new FPV systems and payloads
* Maintain a living FPV tactics repository
6. Training Pipeline
6.1 FPV Operator Course (Initial)
Duration: 3 weeks (intensive)
Content:

* FPV fundamentals (airframes, power, comms)
* Simulator-based flight training
* Live flight and obstacle navigation
* Payload integration and employment
* EW awareness and countermeasures
* Mission planning and debriefing
* Safety and ROE considerations
Output: Qualified FPV Operator (ISR / Strike).
6.2 FPV Team Training
Duration: 1 week
Focus:

* Team drills
* Targeting processes
* Integration with supported units
* Red vs Blue scenarios
* Communications and battle tracking
6.3 FPV Instructor Course
Eligibility: Experienced FPV operators Duration: 2 weeks
Focus:

* Instructional methods
* Standards enforcement
* Safety oversight
* TTP development
6.4 Continuation Training

* Quarterly refreshers
* Scenario-based simulations
* Live-fire / live-effect events (where authorised)
7. Force Generation Model
Initial Objective

* Generate 10 × 10-man FPV teams within 12 months
Manpower Source

* Reserve brigade personnel (primary)
* Selected regular personnel (key roles)
Team Composition

* Team Leader
* Deputy / Planner
* FPV Operators
* Systems / Payload Specialists
* EW / Comms Specialist
* Maintainer
8. Production Model (Organic & Resilient)
8.1 Organic Assembly
ATI-Hub will maintain the capability to:

* Assemble FPV drones from commercial components
* Modify payloads and configurations
* Rapidly replace damaged systems
* Iterate designs based on field feedback
8.2 Production Scale

* Pilot production runs up to 100 units per configuration
* Enables:
   * Rapid fielding
   * Stockpiling
   * Discretion and non-attribution
8.3 Configuration Control

* Standardised components where possible
* Modular payloads
* Version control managed by ATI-Hub
9. Sustainment & Maintenance
Philosophy

* Repair forward where possible
* Low-cost, disposable mindset for airframes
* High-value focus on:
   * Batteries
   * Controllers
   * Payloads
Maintenance Levels

* Operator-level: Basic repairs, swaps
* ATI-Hub-level: Complex repairs, upgrades
* Prime / Industry: As directed by PGIC
10. Employment Concepts
Roles

* Tactical ISR
* Precision strike
* Route reconnaissance
* Overwatch and screening
* Target marking
Integration

* Embedded with manoeuvre units
* Controlled by supported commander
* Integrated into fires and ISR cycles
Experimentation

* Red vs Blue exercises
* Counter-FPV tactics development
* EW and contested environment trials
11. Risk Management
Key Risks

* Operator attrition
* EW vulnerability
* Safety incidents
* Rapid tech obsolescence
Mitigations

* Continuous training pipeline
* Frequent TTP updates
* Modular system design
* Strong safety governance
12. Measures of Effectiveness

* Time to train and deploy teams
* Operator proficiency scores
* Mission success rates
* System loss vs effect achieved
* Adoption by supported units
13. Transition & Growth

* Validated FPV systems may transition to:
   * Wider PG adoption
   * Formal CAPDEV programs
   * Prime-led production
* Program will inform:
   * Doctrine
   * Training design
   * Future UxS capability decisions
14. End State
The FPV & UxS Program delivers a credible, scalable, and resilient capability that enhances PG combat effectiveness, accelerates learning, and serves as a model for future innovation programs under PG Innovation Command.

Proceeding with Deliverable 9 (Final).
Below is the PG Innovation Command – Innovation Charter, written to be authoritative, durable, and protective of the capability over time. This is the document that survives leadership change and prevents the organisation from drifting, being hollowed out, or misused.
PG INNOVATION COMMAND (PGIC)
INNOVATION CHARTER
1. Purpose of the Charter
This Charter formally establishes the mandate, authority, scope, cultural principles, and performance expectations of PG Innovation Command (PGIC) and its execution components, including the All-Domain Technology & Innovation Hub (ATI-Hub).
The Charter exists to:

* Protect innovation tempo and intent
* Prevent role confusion with CAPDEV and procurement
* Provide clarity to commanders, staff, and partners
* Ensure continuity across leadership rotations
* Codify behaviours required for long-term success
2. Mandate
PG Innovation Command is mandated by Presidential Guard Command (PGC) to:
Identify, de-risk, and deliver validated capability options at speed by solving priority operational problems through structured innovation, experimentation, and pathfinding activities.
PGIC is the enterprise authority for innovation within the Presidential Guard.
3. Scope of Authority
PGIC IS AUTHORISED TO:

* Accept and prioritise innovation problem statements
* Allocate resources to approved innovation activities
* Task ATI-Hub and other execution nodes
* Authorise rapid prototyping, trials, and experimentation
* Approve kill / continue / transition decisions
* Engage industry, academia, and SMEs within approved frameworks
* Produce validated recommendations for CAPDEV and acquisition
PGIC IS NOT AUTHORISED TO:

* Conduct or replace formal procurement
* Commit the Presidential Guard to long-term sustainment
* Bypass governance, legal, or security requirements
* Operate outside PGC strategic intent
4. ATI-Hub Authority (Delegated)
ATI-Hub, as a component of PGIC, is authorised to:

* Execute PGIC-approved innovation tasks
* Conduct innovation sprints and trials
* Build and test prototypes
* Conduct limited organic production (0–100 units)
* Develop TTPs, training inputs, and fielding dossiers
* Recommend transition or termination actions
ATI-Hub does not set enterprise priorities or approve scaling beyond delegated limits.
5. Innovation Principles
All PGIC and ATI-Hub personnel will operate under the following principles:

1. Problem First Innovation begins with a real operational problem, not technology availability.
2. People-Centred End users are embedded early and continuously.
3. Speed with Discipline Rapid action is encouraged, but governed by clear decision gates.
4. Early Failure is Success Failure in experimentation is acceptable; failure in fielding is not.
5. Outcome-Driven Value is measured by operational effect, not activity.
6. Pathfinding, Not Procurement Innovation creates options; acquisition delivers scale.
7. Flat Execution, Vertical Accountability Ideas can come from anywhere; accountability remains clear.
6. Cultural Expectations
PGIC culture will be deliberately cultivated and protected.
Expected Behaviours

* Ownership of problems from start to finish
* Willingness to challenge assumptions respectfully
* Collaboration across ranks, trades, and organisations
* Persistence in the face of slow decision cycles
* Professional engagement with external partners
Unacceptable Behaviours

* Innovation theatre without outcomes
* Technology chasing without users
* Risk avoidance that prevents learning
* Bureaucratic delay without justification
* Personality-driven decision-making
7. Operating Boundaries
To maintain focus and credibility:

* PGIC will maintain a balanced innovation portfolio (Three Horizons).
* Projects without a sponsor or operator champion will not proceed.
* Innovation activity will stop or pivot when value is not demonstrated.
* Scaling beyond pilot production requires PGIC and PGC approval.
8. Performance & Metrics
PGIC performance will be assessed using a limited set of meaningful indicators:

* Time from problem acceptance to prototype
* Time from prototype to field trial
* Percentage of projects transitioned or deliberately terminated
* Operator adoption and satisfaction
* Cost and resilience benefits versus alternatives
Metrics exist to enable decision-making, not to drive behaviour distortion.
9. Engagement with Industry & Academia
PGIC will:

* Act as a controlled front door for external engagement
* Use transparent challenge-based approaches where appropriate
* Protect IP, security, and national interests
* Maintain professional, non-commercialised relationships
Industry engagement supports outcomes; it does not define them.
10. Review & Adaptation
This Charter will be:

* Reviewed annually by PGIC
* Endorsed by PGC
* Updated only where required to maintain relevance and effectiveness
Changes to the Charter will not dilute mandate, authority, or principles.
11. Enduring Intent
PG Innovation Command exists to ensure the Presidential Guard:

* Learns faster than its adversaries
* Adapts before threats mature
* Fields capability with confidence and evidence
* Retains sovereignty, discretion, and resilience
* Embeds innovation as a permanent command function, not a temporary initiative
12. Charter Endorsement
This Charter enters into effect upon approval by Presidential Guard Command (PGC).

PRESIDENTIAL GUARD INNOVATION COMMAND (PGIC)

DOCUMENT CONTROL
Title: PG Innovation Command (PGIC) & ATI-Hub Author: [Redacted] Authority: Presidential Guard Command (PGC) Status: Draft for Approval Classification: As Directed
SECTION 1 – EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
Purpose
This document seeks Presidential Guard Command (PGC) approval to establish a PG Innovation Command (PGIC) and, within it, an All-Domain Technology & Innovation Hub (ATI-Hub) as a Desert Warfare execution node. Together, these form a command-led, enterprise innovation system designed to rapidly convert priority operational problems into tested, fieldable capability options.
Problem Statement
The Presidential Guard operates in an environment where threats, technologies, and tactics evolve faster than traditional capability development and procurement cycles. While training and operational excellence are strong, PG lacks a permanent, integrated mechanism to:

* Convert operational problems into solutions at speed
* Experiment and learn safely in realistic environments
* Bridge the gap between prototypes and fielded capability
* Leverage industry and civilian expertise efficiently
* Retain discretion, sovereignty, and resilience in emerging technologies
Proposed Solution
Establish PG Innovation Command (PGIC) under PGC, with ATI-Hub as a core execution component. PGIC governs innovation enterprise-wide; ATI-Hub executes rapid design, experimentation, validation, and limited fielding for desert warfare, UxS, FPV, and discreet effects.
Decision Required
PGC approval to:

1. Establish PG Innovation Command (PGIC)
2. Authorise ATI-Hub as a PGIC execution component
3. Endorse phased 24-month implementation
4. Delegate innovation authorities as defined
SECTION 2 – STRATEGIC CONTEXT
Modern conflict demonstrates that advantage now lies in speed of adaptation, not solely in scale or legacy platforms. Low-cost autonomous systems, FPV drones, rapid prototyping, and decentralised innovation have proven decisive.
To maintain advantage, PG must:

* Learn faster than adversaries
* Experiment safely and often
* Field capability before threats mature
* Reduce reliance on slow, vendor-led cycles
SECTION 3 – COMMAND & CAPABILITY STRUCTURE
Authoritative Structure

```
Presidential Guard Command (PGC)
        │
        └── PG Innovation Command (PGIC)
                │
                └── ATI-Hub (Desert Warfare Execution Node)
```

Role Separation

* PGC: Strategic intent, authority, risk acceptance
* PGIC: Enterprise innovation command, governance, prioritisation, transition
* ATI-Hub: Execution, prototyping, experimentation, validation, pilot fielding
SECTION 4 – PG INNOVATION COMMAND (PGIC) CONOPS
Mission
PGIC governs and enables enterprise innovation across the Presidential Guard in order to deliver validated capability options at speed.
Scope
PGIC:

* Sets innovation priorities
* Manages portfolio (Three Horizons 70/20/10)
* Allocates resources
* Authorises experimentation and trials
* Approves kill / continue / transition decisions
* Interfaces with CAPDEV and acquisition
PGIC does not replace procurement.
Operating Model
Innovation is problem-led, people-centric, and outcome-driven, operating through a structured pipeline from problem definition to transition.
SECTION 5 – ATI-HUB CONOPS
Mission
ATI-Hub executes PGIC-directed innovation through rapid design, prototyping, experimentation, and validation to deliver field-ready options.
Focus Areas

* Desert warfare applications
* UxS (UAS / UGV)
* FPV ISR and strike
* Discreet and low-signature effects
Sub-Elements

* Research & Development Wing
* Test, Evaluation & Experimentation Wing
* UxS & FPV Capability Wing
* Transition & Production Cell
* Engagement & Ecosystem Cell
Operating Environment

* Off-camp innovation facility
* Secure classified annex
* Fabrication labs, FPV lanes, test areas
* Digital innovation tracking environment
SECTION 6 – GOVERNANCE & AUTHORITY MODEL
Decision Rights

* PGC: Strategic priorities, major risk acceptance
* PGIC: Project approval, resourcing, transition decisions
* ATI-Hub: Execution, prototyping, trials, recommendations
Innovation Priorities Board
Chaired by PGIC Commander; approves problems, portfolio balance, transitions, and termination.
Principles

* Command-led, not committee-led
* Flat execution, vertical accountability
* Early failure encouraged, late failure prevented
SECTION 7 – THREE HORIZONS MODEL

* Horizon 1 (70%) – Improve current capability
* Horizon 2 (20%) – Emerging technologies
* Horizon 3 (10%) – Disruptive future concepts
Ensures balance between immediate value and future advantage.
SECTION 8 – END-TO-END INNOVATION PIPELINE

1. Command-endorsed problem
2. Innovation sprint
3. ≤90-day prototype
4. Tactical experimentation (TEX)
5. Transition gate
6. Pilot production (0–100 units)
7. Handover to CAPDEV / prime
SECTION 9 – 24-MONTH IMPLEMENTATION PLAN
Phase 1 (0–6 Months): Pathfinding

* Stand up PGIC and ATI-Hub
* Secure facility
* Launch FPV pilot
* Deliver first prototypes
Phase 2 (6–18 Months): System Build

* Expand staff and infrastructure
* Publish doctrine annexes
* First transitions to CAPDEV
Phase 3 (18–24 Months): Scale

* Increase throughput
* Expand innovation nodes
* Institutionalise capability
SECTION 10 – ORBAT SUMMARY
PGIC

* Commander
* Governance & Portfolio Office
* Transition & CAPDEV Interface
ATI-Hub

* OF-5 Commander
* RDW / TEX / UxS-FPV Wings
* Transition Cell
* Engagement Cell
FPV Teams

* 10 × 10-man teams
* Reserve-enabled
* Modular, task-organised
SECTION 11 – FPV & UXS PROGRAM ANNEX
Purpose
Rapidly generate FPV ISR and strike capability as an early, visible operational win.
Training Pipeline

* FPV Operator Course (3 weeks)
* Team Training
* Instructor Course
* Continuation training
Production

* Organic assembly
* Modular payloads
* Pilot runs up to 100 units
Employment

* Tactical ISR
* Precision strike
* Route reconnaissance
* Red vs Blue experimentation
SECTION 12 – INNOVATION CHARTER
Mandate
Identify, de-risk, and deliver validated capability options at speed.
Principles

* Problem first
* People-centred
* Speed with discipline
* Pathfinding, not procurement
* Outcome-driven
Metrics

* Time to prototype
* Time to field trial
* Transition rate
* Operator adoption
END STATE
PG Innovation Command and ATI-Hub form a durable, enterprise innovation system that enables the Presidential Guard to learn faster than adversaries, field capability at speed, and retain sovereignty and resilience in future conflict.

✅ Simulation Capability Requirements for ITSECC 2025 Vendor Identification
Desert Warfare School – Synthetic Training Modernisation
1. Core Capability Categories to Source
Identify companies that provide solutions across the following domains:
A. Live, Virtual, Constructive (LVC) Integration

* Platforms that link live training, virtual simulation, and constructive (AI-driven) entities.
* Ability to integrate across the school’s existing training streams: individual skills, collective training, SAT cells, and advanced wings.
* Compatible with PG HQ C4I and future OSINT / ISR fusion centre concepts.
2. Individual Skills Simulators
A. Shooting / Weapons Simulation
Seek systems that provide:

* Immersive weapons handling and marksmanship simulation
* Accurate ballistics modelling (wind, elevation, target movement, scopes, NVGs)
* Multi-weapon compatibility:
   * Carbine platforms (5.56)
   * 7.62 rifles
   * GPMG / LMG simulation
   * Pistols
   * Breaching shotguns
* Realistic recoil kits, CO2 / recoil engines, smart magazines
* Multi-lane range capability (10–20 lanes)
* Replication of:
   * Close-quarters battle (CQB)
   * Urban shoots
   * Vehicle-borne firing
   * Desert open-terrain engagements
* After-Action Review (AAR): HD replay, shot traces, hit accuracy, behavioural analytics
* Automatic training programme generation via SAT alignment
Look for companies like VirTra, Bohemia Interactive Simulations, MILO Range, InVeris, etc.
B. Medical Simulation
Requirements:

* Tactical Combat Casualty Care (TCCC) manikins with:
   * Pulses, breathing, bleeding control
   * Penetrating trauma, blast trauma, burns
   * Airway management (NPA, surgical airway)
   * Needle decompression, IV/IO insertion
* Scenario-based training for desert warfare casualties
* Multi-casualty simulation table with wireless control
* Integration with force-on-force casualty effects
* Smart sensors to record treatment steps for AAR
* Remote instructor override/control tablet
* Ruggedised for field deployment at Al Faya training areas
Potential companies: TacMed, CAE Healthcare, Gaumard, Operative Experience.
C. Navigation & Planning Simulation
Capabilities required:

* High-resolution desert terrain models (local UAE/Al Faya/Wadi areas)
* Simulated GPS, map, compass, movement/navigation errors
* Digital planning tools for:
   * Patrol planning
   * RV/ERV planning
   * Route selection
   * Risk and threat overlays
* Integration with:
   * UAV feed simulation
   * Recon cell
   * ISR injects
* Multi-team collaborative mission planning in a virtual space
Vendors: VBS4 by BISim, Mantle, Varjo mixed-reality integrations.
D. Paramotor Simulator
Specialist requirement:

* Virtual take-off, flight, and landing simulation
* Desert wind and turbulence modelling
* Engine failure/emergency drills
* VR headset and integrated throttle/brake controls
* Realistic paraglider wing response
* Training for:
   * Navigation
   * Reconnaissance
   * Operating with weight vests / equipment loads
* AAR that logs flight path and navigation mistakes
Only a few niche companies offer this – may require custom builds or VR integrators.
E. Driving/Vehicle Simulators
Capabilities:

* Light tactical vehicle simulation (MRZR / Hilux / Patriot / NIMR)
* Desert sand driving (soft sand, dunes, rocky wadis)
* Convoy drills, ambush, IED threats
* Night driving with NVGs
* Vehicle gunnery simulation
* Integration into collective virtual scenarios
* Instructor station & AAR suite
* Physical driver capsules with motion seats
Top vendors: FAAC, MILRAD, Bohemia (paired with full-motion VR rigs), Quantum3D.
3. Collective Simulation Systems
A. Virtual Collective Training (Section–Company Level)

* VBS4-grade or Unreal Engine military simulation environments
* Real-time simulation of friendly, enemy, and civilian patterns
* Integration with PG’s emerging Red Force concept
* Ability to simulate:
   * Reconnaissance missions
   * Raids
   * Desert warfare assaults
   * Vehicle movement
   * Fire support integration
* JTAC/close air support simulation (SimCENTRIC, Immersive Wisdom, etc.)
B. C2 & Mission Rehearsal Simulation
Capabilities:

* Virtual TOC (Operations Room)
* Common operational picture
* ISR injects (UAV, SIGINT, OSINT)
* AI-driven enemy dynamics
* Real-time mission rehearsal with AAR for commanders
* Link with reality-based tracking (e.g., PG GPS systems) for hybrid exercises
4. Facility Requirements for Vendor Selection
Ensure vendors can support:
A. Integrated Training Centre Design

* Turnkey design or consultancy
* Wiring, server architecture, networking
* Shock-mounted structures
* Multi-room VR-compatible lighting
* Secure instructor control rooms
* Future growth areas (paramotor wing, SAT cell expansion, Red Force)
B. System Integration & Interoperability

* Open architecture APIs
* Ability to ingest/export data to SAT planning systems
* Cross-linking between weapons, medical, driving, planning sims
* Unified AAR system for all wings of the School
* Dedicated multi-domain server backend
* Compatibility with PG HQ networks
C. Maintenance, Support, and Warranty

* In-country support footprint (UAE preferred)
* Spare parts availability
* Training for PG technicians
* 24/7 remote support capability
* Long-term maintenance contract options
* Rugged, low-maintenance systems suitable for desert environments
5. Evaluation Criteria
Use this matrix during ITSECC:
A. Capability Fit

* Does the product meet the functional need?
* Does it support Desert Warfare scenarios specifically?
B. Scalability

* Can it scale to battalion-level if needed?
* Can modules be upgraded?
C. Integration

* Can it integrate with other PG systems?
* Is data export/AAR cross-compatible?
D. Cost & Lifecycle

* Acquisition cost
* 5–10 year sustainment cost
* Software licensing/support fees
E. Maturity & Track Record

* In-service with militaries?
* Proven training outcomes?
* Reliability in harsh environments?
F. Training & Support

* Does the vendor train instructors?
* Does it provide courseware or only hardware?
6. Questions to Ask Vendors
Use this live at ITSECC:
Technical

* “What engine/environment does your simulation run on?”
* “Can it integrate with VBS4 or Unreal/Unity-based systems?”
* “What AAR capabilities do you provide?”
Operational

* “Have you delivered solutions to desert warfare environments?”
* “Do you support multi-wing training (shooting, medical, driving)?”
* “Can we link your simulator with our live-fire ranges?”
Support

* “Do you have UAE-based or GCC support?”
* “What is your spare parts strategy?”
* “What is your software update and security policy?”
7. Output Options I Can Generate for You

[user] Conduct deep research into VTOL and E-VTOL products that could be used for Military operations. Focus on personal / individual craft, but also look into larger options
Awesome brief—here’s a tight, defense-focused scan of VTOL / eVTOL options that could actually serve military missions. I’ve split it into (A) personal/individual craft and (B) larger platforms, then mapped use-cases, maturity, and key constraints so you can shortlist for demos/trials in desert, littoral, and urban contexts.
Executive takeaways

* Personal lift is real but niche: Turbine jet suits/boards deliver the most raw performance for short, high-impact tasks (VBSS vertical access, cliff/mountain insertions) but have ~5–10 min endurance, training burden, and logistics (fuel/heat) concerns. Multirotor ultralights (electric) are quieter and simpler to fly but limited to ~15–25 min and light payloads.
* Near-term utility at scale is cargo & liaison: Autonomous/hybrid VTOL cargo aircraft (Elroy Air Chaparral) and enclosed-rotor VTOL (Urban Aero/Tactical Robotics Cormorant) are best fits for tactical resupply / CASEVAC in cluttered or denied LZs. Several have U.S. DoD/Agility Prime backing.
* Crewed multiseat eVTOLs are already on USAF ramps: Joby delivered to Edwards AFB under Agility Prime; Archer has a DoD contract up to $142M; BETA ALIA has military airworthiness. These are high-TRL for liaison, medevac (light), logistics, and training support.
A) Personal / individual VTOL options
1) Gravity Industries Jet Suit (turbine, kerosene) — vertical access, VBSS trials

* Demonstrated with Royal Marines / Royal Navy for ship boarding; “not ready yet” for formal adoption but shows clear vertical access advantages. Typical endurance ≲10 min; ~1,000+ bhp; strong training and fitness demands; hot exhaust signature.
* Regular defense demos incl. NATO SAR/mountain warfare; ongoing public demonstrations in 2024–2025.
2) Zapata “Flyboard Air” (turbine hoverboard)

* 5 micro-turbines, ~10 min endurance, very high dash speeds; French DGA previously awarded funding; widely publicized Bastille Day and Channel crossing attempts/success. Operationally demanding; single-point pilot platform.
3) Electric ultralight multicopters (Part-103-style) — fast-learners, low acoustic, short legs

* LIFT HEXA: 18-rotor, amphibious, 1-seat; ~15 min, ~45–63 mph; flown with USAF Agility Prime (Phase III).
* Jetson ONE: ~20 min, software-limited ~102 km/h; 1-seat; simple to fly; limited payload and weather tolerance.
* Pivotal Helix (ex-Opener BlackFly): 1-seat fixed-wing eVTOL; ~20+ miles, ~63 mph; marketed for public safety/defense.
* RYSE Recon: 1-seat, 6 rotors; ~25 miles, ~50 kts; targeted at rangers, ag, security; water ops.
4) High-speed turbine VTOL drone (Mayman Aerospace RAZOR family)

* Jet-propulsion VTOL AUV concept pitched for ISTAR, counter-UAS, strike, cargo; test flights reported 2024–2025; UAE EDGE/SDF support noted publicly. Still early-maturity, but relevant for autonomous high-speed insertions.
5) First-responder tail-sitter eVTOL (Jump Aero JA1 Pulse)

* Single pilot-medic, 250-kt dash, reach 50 km in <8 min; USAF AFWERX TACFI backing; potential dual-use for SOF rapid responder / PJ roles. Limited range/endurance by design (go-fast/short).
Where these shine (personal class)

* Maritime VBSS / vertical access (jet suits/boards).
* Point-to-point scout / courier / MEDEVAC medic-arrival (Pulse / ultralight eVTOL).
* Quiet patrol of short sectors (ultralight e-multicopters) with minimal ground footprint.
B) Larger VTOL / eVTOL with direct military traction
Urban Aeronautics / Tactical Robotics “Cormorant” (enclosed-rotor VTOL, kerosene, uncrewed)

* Designed for tactical resupply & CASEVAC in urban/cluttered terrain; enclosed fans enable operation near buildings/streets; stated 500 kg payload to ~200 km; multiple autonomous flights demonstrated. High potential for confined LZ MEDEVAC.
Elroy Air “Chaparral” (hybrid-electric autonomous cargo VTOL)

* Nominal payload 136 kg (max 227 kg), nominal range ~482 km, 8.5 hr endurance reported; explicitly targeting military resupply under Agility Prime/DoD interest. Swappable cargo pods, autonomous ops.
Joby S4 (crewed eVTOL)

* Delivered first aircraft to Edwards AFB under $131M Agility Prime; ongoing USAF testing and CONOPS exploration (liaison, logistics, training, low-sig mobility). Low acoustic signature is a strength.
Archer “Midnight” (crewed eVTOL)

* USAF/DoD contracts up to $142M; partnering with Anduril on a defense variant/hybrid concept. Suited to short-range lift, base-to-base liaison, and potentially light CASEVAC where infrastructure exists.
BETA “ALIA-250” (crewed eVTOL/CTOL family)

* USAF Agility Prime flight testing and military airworthiness; strong logistics/liaison profile and an expanding charging network; payload and range figures suitable for base sustainment and light CASEVAC.
Mission fit (quick matrix)
MissionBest-fit classesNotesVBSS / vertical accessJet Suit / Flyboard60–90-second ship-to-deck transitions; endurance margins tight; hot exhaust signature & training burden. Desert cliff/mountain access, SARJet Suit, HEXA/ultralight eVTOLJet suit fast but noisy/hot; eVTOL quieter, safer near personnel but much slower. Medic-first arrival (PJ/CLS), QRF courierJump Aero Pulse, ultralight e-multicoptersPulse trades endurance for speed; multicopters simple to stage and recover. Tactical resupply (50–300 km), denied/confined LZsCormorant, Elroy ChaparralEnclosed rotors (Cormorant) for street canyons; Chaparral modular pods for repetitive sorties. Light CASEVACCormorant, crewed eVTOL (Joby/Archer/BETA)Cormorant for confined LZ extraction; crewed eVTOL for base-to-base with med support. Base liaison / staff mobilityJoby / Archer / BETAHighest TRL in DoD; low acoustic, simple ops on prepared pads.
Practical constraints you’ll have to account for

* Endurance vs. payload (battery): Personal e-multicopters are 15–25 min class—fine for short hops, not patrol. Hybrid or turbine options extend range but raise acoustic/thermal signatures.
* Signature management: Turbine devices are loud/hot (IR risk); eVTOL prop noise is lower but not stealthy, and prop downwash/dust is material in desert.
* Training & human factors: Jet suits demand strength and specific technique; ultralight eVTOL are simpler but still require conversion training and envelope discipline.
* Weather & EW: Crosswinds/dust hurt multicopter efficiency; autonomous systems must be hardened against GNSS denial / datalink interference—programmatic requirement for military adoption. (Inference based on operational norms; verify in trials.)
* Regulatory & airworthiness: DoD’s AFWERX/Agility Prime pathway is the most credible route for near-term operational experimentation and waivered ops.
* Logistics: For electric fleets, forward charging is the chokepoint; BETA is fielding a DoD-compatible charging footprint in CONUS—concept is replicable OCONUS with deployable gensets/battery trailers.
Shortlist (by role) to move into PG-style trials
Personal access (urban/maritime)

* Gravity Jet Suit for VBSS / vertical access proof-of-concept (PTO/RA, safety, SOPs).
* LIFT HEXA or Pivotal Helix for quiet, short-range overwatch/liaison where low noise and simplicity matter.
Autonomous resupply & CASEVAC

* Urban Aero/Tactical Robotics Cormorant for confined LZ CASEVAC and resupply in urban canyons.
* Elroy Air Chaparral for podded cargo logistics (50–200 kg class) across 100–400+ km.
Crewed liaison / training / med support

* Joby S4, Archer Midnight, BETA ALIA via a defense evaluation pathway; mature OEM support, established USAF testing.
High-speed autonomous strike/ISTAR (experimental)

* Mayman RAZOR (UAE-linked backing reported) for exploratory trials where speed/agility outweigh endurance; treat as early-TRL.
Spec snapshots (indicative, vendor-stated/industry-reported)

* HEXA (LIFT) — 1p, ~15 min, ~45–63 mph; 18 rotors; amphibious; Agility Prime testing.
* Jetson ONE — 1p, ~20 min, ~102 km/h; 86–115 kg AUW; ultralight simplicity.
* Pivotal Helix — 1p, ~20+ mi, ~63 mph; fixed-wing eVTOL; defense/public safety marketed.
* Jump Aero JA1 Pulse — 1p+kit, 250 kt dash, 50 km in <8 min; USAF TACFI; tail-sitter.
* Gravity Jet Suit — ~10 min endurance; trials with Royal Marines/RN for VBSS.
* Zapata Flyboard Air — 5 turbines, ~10 min; DGA backing; high speed; demanding to operate.
* Urban Aero Cormorant — uncrewed; 500 kg payload ~200 km; enclosed rotors for confined LZs.
* Elroy Air Chaparral — hybrid-electric; 136–227 kg payload, ~482 km nominal range, 8.5 h endurance (reported).
* Joby S4 — USAF Agility Prime; aircraft delivered to Edwards AFB for testing.
* Archer Midnight — DoD up to $142M; Anduril defense partnership/hybrid VTOL concept.
* BETA ALIA — USAF airworthiness & trials; expanding charging network.
What I’d do next (fast, low-risk, high-insight)

1. Two-lane demo program (90 days)
   * Lane 1 (access/first-arrival): Invite Gravity (VBSS vertical access drills over water) + LIFT HEXA / Pivotal Helix (short-hop overwatch/liaison).
   * Lane 2 (cargo/CASEVAC): Run desert and urban-canyon scenarios with Elroy Chaparral (podded resupply) and request Cormorant confined-LZ medevac demo package.
2. Operational envelopes & safety cases: Dust/DVE testing, hot-soak, battery derate curves at >40 °C, GNSS-denied nav profiles, and downwash effects on troops/equipment. (Operational test focus)
3. Sustainment package: For electric fleets, procure deployable chargers/gensets + battery spares; for turbine devices, define fuel staging, PPE, and hot-exhaust SOPs. (Programmatic)
4. Doctrine & training: Build mini-SOPs for VBSS with jet suit lead climbers; medic-first arrival CONOPS; pod swap-times and LZ discipline for Chaparral-class platforms. (TTPs)

[user] Provide a capability need statement for a tactical resupply drone
Capability Need Statement — Tactical Resupply Drone
1. Title
Tactical Resupply Uncrewed Aerial System (TR-UAS)
2. Capability Need Summary
A reliable, survivable, and easily deployable tactical resupply drone to deliver critical supplies (ammunition, medical aid, batteries, sensors, specialist spares) from unit-level logistics nodes to dispersed combat elements in austere desert and littoral environments. The system must increase sustainment reach, reduce ground convoy risk, shorten resupply timelines, and enable continuous operations in degraded or contested environments.
3. Operational Problem
Ground resupply in expeditionary/desert operations is slow, exposes convoys to ambush/IED threat, and is constrained by terrain, restricted lines-of-communication, and weather (dust storms, high heat). Small units operating beyond line-of-sight or inside complex terrain (urban edges, canyons, wadis) require rapid, on-demand delivery of small-to-medium payloads to maintain tempo and reduce mission aborts. Existing heavy rotary/wing resupply assets are inefficient for frequent, low-volume tactical deliveries.
4. Desired Operational Effects

* Reduce time-to-resupply for frontline teams from hours to minutes.
* Reduce exposed personnel and vehicle risk by enabling remote resupply.
* Increase mission endurance (sustainment of combat effect) without additional ground logistics tail.
* Provide precision delivery to pre-coordinated or ad-hoc drop points with minimal manpower overhead.
5. Key Employment Concepts

* Unit-level request → launched from forward arming/refuel/logistics node or vehicle → autonomous or supervised flight → precision delivery (soft-drop, winch/retract, or landing) → return-to-base or expendable options.
* Rapid re-tasking via secure datalink to support dynamic battlefields.
* Integration with SAT/mission planning tools and force C2 for tasking and deconfliction.
6. Minimum Mandatory Requirements (Threshold)
These are non-negotiable for initial fielding.

* Payload: 10 kg net usable payload (modular cargo pods).
* Range: 20 km one-way (40 km round trip) with payload under typical desert conditions.
* Endurance: ≥30 minutes with 10 kg payload (cruise).
* Cruise Speed: ≥70 km/h; max speed ≥100 km/h.
* Delivery Modes: At least two: precision soft-drop (GPS-guided) and winch delivery to stationary or stationary-moving recipients.
* Navigation: GNSS with inertial navigation backup and terrain/vision-aided navigation for GPS-denied environments.
* Datalink: Encrypted, anti-jam capable beyond-line-of-sight (BLOS) or LOS with relay option.
* Environmental Tolerance: Operate in +50°C ambient, sand/dust ingestion tolerance, wind up to 30 km/h sustained, and survival through short-duration dust storms.
* Launch/Recovery: Vehicle/van/flat-packable launch & rapid deployable recovery options; no requirement for prepared runway.
* Operator Requirement: Crew of ≤2 trained operators for routine operations; single-operator mission control for common profiles.
* Logistics: Ground deployable ground support equipment (GSE) that fits within tactical vehicles (e.g., Land Cruiser / MRZR / Polaris MRZR footprint).
* Safety/Standards: Fail-safe return-to-home (RTH), safe descent in failure modes, and compliance with applicable airspace and safety rules for military operations.
7. Desired Capabilities (Objective)

* Payload: 20 kg payload option (modular).
* Range/Endurance: 50 km one-way / ≥90 minutes endurance with smaller payloads.
* Autonomy: High autonomy: automated replan, obstacle avoidance, dynamic routing, multi-aircraft swarm logistics coordination.
* Survivability: Low-observable signature, radar cross-section reduction, and counter-EW resilience.
* Rapid Reload: Ground reload/re-arm cycle ≤10 minutes with minimal tooling.
* Hot & Cold Weather: Operability down to -10°C and up to +55°C.
* Interoperability: Standardized interfaces for integration with unit mission planning tools, C2, and logistics trackers (APIs).
* Modularity: Plug-and-play pods for medevac (small casualty extraction), ISR sensors, fuel cell/battery recharge capsule.
* Precision Landing: Autonomous vertical landing on small prepared pads or vehicle decks.
* Night Ops: Full night capability with IR/lidar aiding.
* Safety: Parachute for airframe in catastrophic failure.
8. Constraints & Assumptions

* System will operate primarily in austere desert/littoral environments with limited forward maintenance infrastructure.
* Airspace will be contested with potential GPS degradation and limited BLOS datalink support; measures for resilience assumed.
* Budget and manpower limitations favor small, modular systems over large, platform-specific solutions.
9. Measures of Effectiveness (MoE)

* Time-to-delivery: % of requests delivered within target time (e.g., ≤20 minutes for point-to-point ≤10 km).
* Mission success rate: % of sorties that deliver cargo within acceptable tolerance.
* Sortie rate: Average deliveries per launch node per 24 hours.
* Risk reduction: Reduction in convoy exposure hours / reduction in ground resupply vehicle km.
* Logistics cost-per-kg: Comparative metric against ground convoy and manned rotary lift.
* Availability: Mission-capable rate (≥85% target).
10. Acceptance Criteria / Key Performance Parameters (KPPs)

* Demonstrated delivery of 10 kg to a 5 m x 5 m target from 20 km range in operational environmental conditions in at least 10 consecutive sorties.
* Return-to-base or controlled recovery in >95% of non-catastrophic failure sorties.
* Reload/recycle time ≤15 minutes under field conditions.
* Operator training requirement ≤ 40 hours initial plus standard recurrent currency.
* Ability to integrate with unit C2 and logistics tracking APIs.
11. Training & Personnel

* Operator course (initial): theory, practical launch/recovery, emergency procedures, EW/GPS denial drills — target ≤40 hours.
* Maintainer course: Level-1 field repair & module swaps — target ≤24 hours.
* Minimal additional manpower: capable of being fielded by existing logistics elements with modest additional manpower.
12. Sustainment & Logistics Support

* Common modular spare parts, swappable batteries/power units, and depot-level repair plan.
* Field support package sized to support a six-airframe squadron for 30 days.
* Battery/energy supply consideration for hot climates and fast recharge/replace options.
13. Safety, Legal & Regulatory Considerations

* Compliance with applicable national/military airspace regulations and local airspace coordination procedures.
* Clear rules of engagement for operations in contested/denied environments, including propagation of loss-of-control and ordnance-free payload rules.
* Biomedical handling SOPs for medical payloads (if used).
14. Risk Assessment (Top Risks)

* Electronic warfare (EW): GPS/datalink jamming causing mission failure. Mitigation: inertial nav, anti-jam datalinks, preprogrammed fail-safes.
* Environmental wear: Sand ingestion, thermal stress reducing MTBF. Mitigation: hardened filters, thermal management.
* Logistics tail: Battery/pod resupply complexity. Mitigation: modular swappable packs and standardized batteries.
* Airspace deconfliction with manned aviation. Mitigation: integrated C2, defined corridors and procedures.
15. Prioritization for Acquisition

1. Survivable navigation & resilient datalink (mandatory).
2. 10 kg payload, 20 km range, reliable precision delivery modes (mandatory).
3. Rapid reload & simple field maintenance (mandatory).
4. Improved range/payload/autonomy options (objective for later blocks).
16. Incremental Development & Fielding Path

* Block 0 (Proof of Concept): 10 kg payload, 20 km range, basic GPS/INS navigation, soft-drop, manual operator control, deployable from trucks.
* Block 1 (Initial Operational Capability): Hardened against dust/heat, anti-jam datalink, winch delivery, operator/maintainer courses.
* Block 2 (Full Capability): Enhanced autonomy, swarm logistics, 20–50 kg scalable pods, precision landing and medevac option.
If you want, I can convert this into a one-page brief for senior leadership (PPT slide) with a short recommended acquisition approach and a draft cost/benefit snapshot tailored to desert operations. Which format do you prefer?
Please provide a short paragraph on concept of employment 
The tactical resupply drone is employed as a front-line logistics multiplier: unit-level requests are queued through local C2 or a logistics node, a nearby launcher (vehicle, trailer or portable rack) dispatches an autonomous or supervised sortie, and the drone conducts a low-signature, GPS/INS-guided approach to deliver modular cargo via soft-drop, winch, or precision landing. Missions are taskable on-demand or pre-planned for routine sustainment, with secure datalink re-tasking to support shifting tactics and emergency casualties. Deconfliction with manned air assets, EW-resilient navigation modes, and fail-safe recovery behaviours (RTH, controlled descent) ensure resilience in contested or GPS-denied environments, while rapid reload and simple field maintenance keep the system cycling to sustain tempo without increasing the ground logistics footprint.

**Conversation 7: Capability Need Statement — Tactical Resupply Drone (TR-UAS)**

A formal Capability Need Statement was developed for a Tactical Resupply Uncrewed Aerial System. Key threads:

**Operational Problem:** Ground resupply in desert/expeditionary environments is slow, exposes convoys to threat, and is constrained by terrain. Small dispersed units need rapid, on-demand delivery of small-to-medium payloads.

**Key Employment Concept:** Unit request → launch from forward logistics node or vehicle → autonomous/supervised flight → precision delivery (soft-drop, winch, or landing) → RTB or expendable option. Integrated with C2 and mission planning tools.

**Threshold Requirements (Non-Negotiable):**
- 10 kg payload, 20 km one-way range, ≥30 min endurance, ≥70 km/h cruise
- Two delivery modes (soft-drop + winch), GNSS + INS backup, encrypted anti-jam datalink
- Operable at +50°C, sand/dust tolerance, winds to 30 km/h
- ≤2 operators, vehicle-deployable GSE, fail-safe RTH

**Objective Capabilities:** 20 kg payload, 50 km range, high autonomy/obstacle avoidance, low-observable signature, ≤10 min reload, swarm logistics coordination, plug-and-play pods (medevac, ISR, fuel cell)

**Measures of Effectiveness:** Time-to-delivery, mission success rate, sortie rate, convoy risk reduction, cost-per-kg, ≥85% availability

**Incremental Fielding Path:** Block 0 (PoC) → Block 1 (IOC, hardened/anti-jam) → Block 2 (full autonomy, 20–50 kg pods, medevac)

**Concept of Employment (Short Para):** Front-line logistics multiplier; unit-level requests queued through C2/logistics node; autonomous/supervised sortie from vehicle/trailer launcher; low-signature GPS/INS approach; modular cargo delivery; EW-resilient navigation; fail-safe recovery; rapid reload maintains tempo without growing ground logistics footprint
